Towards a Unified Analysis of Neural Networks in Nonparametric Instrumental Variable Regression: Optimization and Generalization
Zonghao Chen, Atsushi Nitanda, Arthur Gretton, Taiji Suzuki

Abstract
We establish the first global convergence result of neural networks for two stage least squares (2SLS) approach in nonparametric instrumental variable regression (NPIV). This is achieved by adopting a lifted perspective through mean-field Langevin dynamics (MFLD), unlike standard MFLD, however, our setting of 2SLS entails a bilevel optimization problem in the space of probability measures. To address this challenge, we leverage the penalty gradient approach recently developed for bilevel optimization which formulates bilevel optimization as a Lagrangian problem. This leads to a novel fully first-order algorithm, termed F 2 BMLD . Apart from the convergence bound, we further provide a generalization bound, revealing an inherent trade-off in the choice of the Lagrange multiplier between optimization and statistical guarantees. Finally, we empirically validate the effectiveness of the proposed method on an offline reinforcement learning benchmark.



1
Introduction

Instrumental variable regression is a method of identifying and estimating the causal effect of the treatment
A
on the outcome
Y
based on observational data even in the presence of unobserved confounding
(
70
)
.
This is achieved by leveraging a valid instrumental variable
W
which only influences the outcome
Y
via the treatment
A
, known as the
exclusion restriction
; and is independent of the unobserved confounder
U
, known as
unconfoundedness
. For instance, if one would like to identify the causal effect of smoking
A
on the risk of lung disease
Y
which may be potentially confounded by one’s occupation and early childhood environment, the cigarette cost
W
would be a valid instrument as it only affects the risk of lung disease
Y
via smoking
A

(
43
)
.

Nonparametric instrumental variable regression
(NPIV) has gained popularity for its flexibility over parametric or semiparametric models when such structures are not warranted
(
56
;
34
)
.
NPIV can be formulated as the following structural equation:

\displaystyle Y=h_{\circ}(A)+U,\quad\mathbb{E}[U\mid W]=0,

(1)

where
h_{\circ}
, referred to as the
structural function
, is the primary object of interest.
Similar formulation also arises in the context of nonparametric proximal causal learning
(
76
)
, offline policy evaluation in reinforcement learning
(
18
)
and more general ill-posed inverse problems
(
12
)
.
Denote the data generating distribution as
P
over
(A,W,Y)
with marginals
P_{A},P_{W},P_{AW},P_{WY}
.
Conditioning both sides of Eq. (
1
) on the instrument
W
, NPIV can be cast as the following integral equation:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[Y\mid W]=(Th_{\circ})(W),

(2)

where
T:L^{2}(P_{A})\to L^{2}(P_{W})
is the conditional expectation operator defined by
Tf=\mathbb{E}[f(A)\mid W]
.
Estimating
h_{\circ}
thus amounts to inverting this operator in Eq. (
2
). In practice, the operator
T
is unknown and must be estimated from data. Moreover, even if
T
were known, its inverse is typically unbounded, rendering recovery of
h_{\circ}
an
ill-posed inverse problem
.

A widely used approach for solving Eq. (
1
) is two-stage least squares (2SLS) regression. Originally developed for linear models in both stages, 2SLS has since been extended to nonlinear settings with fixed feature maps, such as sieve
(
7
)
and reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces
(
68
)
.
2SLS consists of two successive least-squares regressions, possibly using two distinct set of i.i.d. samples from
P
: in the first stage, one estimates the conditional expectation operator
T
(or a suitable surrogate, such as the conditional mean embedding), and in the second stage, one estimates the structural function
h_{\circ}
using the conditional feature map estimated in the first stage. Under a so-called measure of ill-posedness on
T
, both kernel 2SLS and sieve 2SLS are minimax optimal for Besov targets
h_{\circ}

(
53
;
14
;
15
)
.

More recently, motivated by the expressive power of deep learning,
85
proposed deep feature instrumental variable (DFIV) regression, which replaces fixed feature maps in 2SLS with adaptive, data-driven neural network representations.
DFIV has demonstrated superior empirical performances over conventional fixed feature 2SLS on several instrumental variable benchmarks.
From a statistical perspective,
37
has established that DFIV is minimax optimal for learning Besov
h_{\circ}
under measure of ill-posedness condition on
T
, provided that the global minimizers of both stages can indeed be attained.
However, this leaves the theory only
half complete
. While the statistical guarantees are now well understood, the
optimization
side remains unresolved. In contrast to fixed-feature 2SLS methods, whose solutions admit closed-form expressions in both stages, DFIV does not enjoy such tractability: identifying global minimizers is an open and challenging problem.
The challenges are mainly two-fold: 1) the inherent non-convexity with respect to neural network parameters and 2) the intrinsic
bilevel optimization
structure of DFIV.
Furthermore, the DFIV algorithm proposed by
85
requires solving a ridge regression problem with the learned neural network features, which incurs a potentially elevated cubic cost due to matrix inversion and requires a large batch size to have stable training dynamics.

In this paper, we propose a new 2SLS algorithm, termed
fully first-order bilevel mean-field Langevin dynamics
(
F
2
BMLD
), for solving Eq. (
1
) with adaptive neural network features.
As the name suggests, our algorithm builds on mean-field Langevin dynamics (MFLD), whose finite-particle and time-discretized implementations correspond precisely to noisy gradient descent training of two-layer neural networks
(
52
;
73
;
59
)
. MFLD offers a
lifted
perspective: noisy gradient descent can be interpreted as optimization in the space of probability measures
\mathcal{P}
with a
convex
objective, which enables MFLD to achieve fast non-asymptotic global convergence guarantees
(
57
)
, thereby addressing the first challenge.

Under this lifted perspective, however, the second challenge becomes more severe: the bilevel optimization problem now needs to be solved in the space of probability measures. The two most common approaches to bilevel optimization—implicit gradient methods
(
31
)
and explicit gradient methods
(
29
;
50
)
—become ineffective in this setting: the former requires second-order derivatives in
\mathcal{P}
that are difficult to implement in practice (see
Section

A.2
), while the latter relies on automatic differentiation, which does not extend naturally to probability measures.
To overcome this difficulty, we propose
F
2
BMLD
which leverages the recent reformulation of bilevel optimization as a constrained optimization problem, and consequently as a Lagrangian problem
(
67
;
41
)
. The benefit of such a reformulation is that it only requires
first-order
derivatives, which allow a tractable finite-particle implementation in practice.

Our contributions in this paper can be summarized as follows:

1.

We prove that the proposed algorithm
F
2
BMLD
can indeed find the globally optimal weights when solving Eq. (
1
) via 2SLS with adaptive two-layer neural network features. Our proof thus completes the optimization side of the theoretical explanation for why adaptive features yield superior empirical performance over fixed-feature 2SLS.
The convergence results of
F
2
BMLD
go beyond direct applications of existing MFLD results, due to
the
nested
dependence between the solutions of two levels induced by the bilevel structure.
Such
nested
dependence makes the upper-level objective smooth yet
non-convex
—a regime that lies outside the standard convex setting where MFLD applies.
This non-convexity poses a significant challenge, yet we are able to establish convergence by imposing an additional mild constraint on the regularization.
This completes the theoretical explanation for why neural network adaptive features outperform fixed-feature 2SLS.

2.

We prove a generalization bound on the minimizer found by
F
2
BMLD
when the loss objectives are estimated with finite i.i.d. samples from
P
in both stages. By contrasting the generalization bound and the convergence bound, we identify a trade-off on the effect of the Lagrange multiplier
\lambda
. For the optimization bound, smaller values of
\lambda
are preferable, as they yield a ‘less non-convex’ objective and a smaller Lipschitz constant, thereby reducing the time-discretization error.
In contrast, for the generalization bound, larger values of
\lambda
are favorable, since they make the Lagrangian problem more faithful to the original bilevel optimization problem.

3.

We empirically evaluate
F
2
BMLD
on offline policy evaluation, a challenging reinforcement learning task. The results show that
F
2
BMLD
matches, and in some cases surpasses, the performance of DFIV, the current state-of-the-art 2SLS regression method for NPIV. Beyond accuracy,
F
2
BMLD
offers two additional practical benefits: (i) it exhibits more stable training dynamics, since it relies solely on first-order derivatives, in contrast to DFIV which requires backpropagating through the ridge regression solution; and (ii) it can be trained with a smaller batch size than DFIV, thereby reducing the memory overhead for large-scale models.

Structure of the paper:

This paper is organized as follows.
Section

2
and
Section

3
provide background on two-stage least squares (2SLS) regression and mean-field Langevin dynamics (MFLD). In
Section

4
, we introduce a lifted perspective on 2SLS regression with two-layer neural network features, establish the existence of an optimal solution to such a lifted problem, and present our fully first-order algorithm
F
2
BMLD
.
Section

5
proves the
convergence
of
F
2
BMLD
to the global optimal solution, while
Section

6
analyzes its
generalization
with access only to finite i.i.d. samples in both stages.
Section

7
reports empirical results on offline policy evaluation. Finally,
Section

8
concludes the paper. Detailed proofs of the theorems are provided in
Section

9
.

1.1
Related work

NPIV and 2SLS:

Two-stage least squares regression (2SLS) with fixed basis functions has been widely used for NPIV problems in economics, encompassing both classical sieve basis
(
56
;
7
)
and more recent reproducing kernel Hilbert space (RKHS) estimators with infinite dimensional basis
(
68
;
53
)
. These methods admit tractable closed-form solutions and enjoy well-understood statistical guarantees, including minimax optimal rates of convergence. Building on advances in deep learning,
86
;
85
proposed deep feature instrumental variable (DFIV) regression which uses adaptive neural network features in 2SLS, demonstrating superior empirical performance over fixed-basis counterparts. Subsequently,
37
established its minimax optimal statistical properties. However, a corresponding optimization theoretic understanding remains largely absent in the literature, especially given the non-convexity of the loss with respect to the neural network parameters. Neural networks have also been employed in alternative algorithms to solve NPIV
(
32
;
26
;
6
;
83
;
71
)
, but these approaches also lack optimization theory.

NPIV in offline reinforcement learning:
The Bellman equation in offline reinforcement learning takes the same form as NPIV in Eq. (
1
), a connection first noted by
11
. We elaborate on this correspondence in
Section

7
. With the recent adoption of neural networks in NPIV, several of these methods have also been adapted to offline reinforcement learning
(
18
;
16
;
86
)
. In this setting, adaptive features such as neural networks are generally preferred over fixed basis, due to the more complex relationships among reinforcement learning variables compared to standard causal inference benchmarks. Furthermore,
46
and
5
analyzed offline reinforcement learning under unobserved confounding on the action, relying on additional instrumental variables for identification. Their resulting structural equations remain analogous to Eq. (
1
).

Mean field Langevin dynamics:

Through the mean-field perspective on the two-layer neural networks, optimization dynamics can be lifted from the parameter space to the space of probability distributions
(
58
;
22
;
52
;
69
;
65
;
20
)
. This reformulation enables global convergence guarantees for gradient descent under suitable conditions
(
22
;
52
)
. Mean-field Langevin dynamics (MFLD)
(
35
)
, a noisy variant of gradient descent, also benefits from this viewpoint; the proximal Gibbs analysis and uniform log-Sobolev inequality (LSI) yield exponential convergence of MFLD under milder assumptions
(
59
;
23
)
. Early analyses of MFLD were restricted to the mean-field limit, leaving the quantitative computational complexity of finite-particle system largely open, although
59
incorporated a time-discretization error via a one-step interpolation argument
(
77
)
.
13
;
73
established the fully time-and space-discretized guarantee, which proved the propagation of chaos
(
74
)
to control the finite-particle approximation error. Fully exploiting the convexity of the objective,
60
further refined the particle approximation (space-discretization) analysis by eliminating the dependence on the LSI-constant, achieving improved quantitative complexity in combination with uniform-in-
N
LSI
(
39
;
21
)
. More recently,
57
provided a direct analysis of MFLD that preserves this improved particle complexity while inheriting the convergence rate in time from the mean-field limit dynamics.

Another important line of research is the extension of MFLD to min-max optimization problems under double-loop schemes
(
81
;
49
;
38
;
42
)
.
Our proposed
F
2
BMLD
in
Section

4
is also a double-loop method, but instead addresses a min–min (bilevel) optimization problem, as reviewed next.

Bilevel optimization:
Bilevel optimization seeks to minimize an upper-level objective that depends implicitly on the solution of a lower-level problem
(
25
)
. A key difficulty is that the lower-level solution is defined only through its optimality conditions, so computing gradients with respect to the upper-level variable requires differentiating through these conditions. Two common approaches are: (i) explicit gradient methods, which treat the lower-level solution as the trajectory of a dynamical system and compute gradients via automatic differentiation
(
50
;
29
;
9
)
; and (ii) implicit gradient methods, which employ the implicit function theorem to derive closed-form expressions for the associated gradients
(
63
;
31
;
33
;
36
;
84
;
2
;
64
)
. See
48
for a review.

In this work, adopting a lifted mean-field perspective, we encounter bilevel optimization over the space of probability measures
\mathcal{P}
. In this setting, the standard approaches above do not apply: automatic differentiation tools are not available over
\mathcal{P}
, and higher-order derivatives are generally intractable (see
Section

A.2
). An alternative is offered by penalty-based (or value-function) methods, which reformulate bilevel problems as single-level constrained optimization problems
(
87
;
47
;
41
)
. This reformulation is particularly appealing here as it enables optimization with
only first-order
information.

Two closely related works are
51
and
30
; the former studies optimization through a sampling process and the latter studies deep equilibrium models over distributional inputs. Both are formulated as bilevel optimization problems, yet, in which only the lower-level problems are defined over
\mathcal{P}
.
Also relevant are the works of
82
;
4
, which consider two layer neural network training as optimization in the space of
signed measures
, which can in turn be recast as bilevel optimization over probability measures. Their lower-level problem admits a closed-form solution, however, which makes the problem substantially simpler.

1.2
Notations:

Let
A
and
W
be random variables on
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d_{a}}
and
\mathcal{W}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d_{w}}
, respectively.
We use boldface
\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{A}
and
\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{W}
to denote their realizations, which also serve as inputs to the neural network.
We use plain symbols
x,z
for neural network parameters.
Ent denotes the negative entropy of a probability measure
\mu
that admits a density function:
\mathrm{Ent}(\mu)=\int\log\mu(x)\mu(x)\mathrm{d}x
.

\mathcal{N}(v,\Sigma)
denotes a Gaussian distribution with mean
v
and covariance
\Sigma
.

\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
denotes the set of real-valued signed measures on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with finite total variation.
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
denotes the set of probability measures on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
denotes the set of probability measures on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with finite second moment.
For any
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
L^{2}(\mu)
is the Hilbert space of (equivalence class of) functions
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\int|f|^{2}d\mu<\infty
.
The symbol
\mathrm{Id}
denotes the identity, which by context may refer either to the finite dimensional identity matrix or the identity operator.

The following divergences between two probability measures
\nu
and
\mu
will be used extensively in this paper. 1. KL denotes the Kullback-Leibler divergence
\mathrm{KL}(\nu,\mu)=\int\log(\frac{\mathrm{d}\nu}{\mathrm{d}\mu})\mathrm{d}\nu
when
\mu
is absolutely continuous with
\nu
and
+\infty
otherwise.
2.
W_{2}(\nu,\mu)
denotes the Wasserstein-2 distance between
\nu
and
\mu
.
3.
\mathrm{FI}(\nu,\mu)
denotes the Fisher divergence which is the squared
L^{2}(\nu)
norm of the difference between the respective score functions.
4.
\mathrm{TV}(\nu,\mu)
denotes the total variation distance.

2
Two-stage Least Squares Regression (2SLS)

In this section, we first review two-stage least squares (2SLS) with fixed features and then with adaptive neural network features.

Fixed feature 2SLS regression

Given two sets of fixed feature functions
\psi(\mathbf{a}),\phi(\mathbf{w})
—e.g splines
(
7
)
and reproducing kernel feature maps
(
68
)
—2SLS performs two successive least squares regressions. Stage I regression targets the
conditional mean embedding
(CME)
\mathbf{w}\mapsto\mathbb{E}[\psi(A)\mid W=\mathbf{w}]
, which acts as a surrogate of the conditional expectation operator
T
. Specifically, for any
f
in the linear span of
\psi
, i.e.,
f(\mathbf{a})=u^{\top}\psi(\mathbf{a})
, we have
u^{\top}\mathbb{E}[\psi(A)\mid W=\mathbf{w}]=\mathbb{E}[f(A)\mid W=\mathbf{w}]=(Tf)(\mathbf{w})
.
The CME is parameterized as a linear function of another set of features
\phi
, i.e.,
\mathbb{E}[\psi(A)\mid W=\mathbf{w}]=V\phi(\mathbf{w})
with a Hilbert-Schmidt operator
V:\textrm{span}(\phi)\to\textrm{span}(\psi)
, which can be learned via the following vector-valued ridge regression:

\displaystyle\hat{V}=\arg\min_{V}\frac{1}{2m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left[\|\psi(\mathbf{a}_{i})-V\phi(\mathbf{w}_{i})\|^{2}\right]+\zeta_{1}\|V\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^{2}.

(3)

Here,
\zeta_{1}>0
is the stage I regularization parameter,
\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{HS}}
denotes the Hilbert–Schmidt norm, and
\{\mathbf{w}_{i},\mathbf{a}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
are
m
i.i.d. samples from
P_{WA}
.
Then, stage II regression targets the structural function
h_{\circ}
via another ridge regression:

\displaystyle\hat{u}=\arg\min_{u}\frac{1}{2n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[(\mathbf{y}_{i}-u^{\top}\hat{V}\phi(\mathbf{w}_{i}))^{2}\right]+\zeta_{2}\|u\|^{2}.

(4)

Here,
\zeta_{2}>0
is the stage II regularization parameter,
\|u\|
denotes the
\ell_{2}
-norm or RKHS norm when appropriate and
\{\mathbf{w}_{i},\mathbf{y}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
are
n
i.i.d. samples from
P_{WY}
.
In contrast to standard non-parametric regression, 2SLS replaces the feature
\psi(A)
with
\hat{V}\phi(W)
—an estimate of the conditional mean embedding from Stage I. This substitution arises from conditioning on the instrument
W
as a means of adjusting for the unobserved confounder
U
as in Eq. (
2
).
The final estimator for the structural function
h_{\circ}
is given by
\hat{h}(\mathbf{a})=\hat{u}^{\top}\psi(\mathbf{a})
.

Deep feature instrumental variable regression

Rather than using fixed feature functions
\phi,\psi
,
deep feature instrumental variable
(DFIV) regression proposes to use data-adaptive features
\phi,\psi
learned by deep neural networks.
Compared against 2SLS with fixed features, DFIV has achieved better empirical performance
(
85
)
.
The original DFIV parameterizes the features
\phi,\psi
with deep neural networks and proposes to solve the linear coefficients
V
and
u
(in Eq. (
3
) and Eq. (
4
)) via closed-form ridge regression, such procedure incurs a cost cubic in the feature dimensions.
Instead, following
37
, we propose an alternative equivalent formulation which approximates
h_{\circ}
directly with a single neural network
h_{\theta_{a}}(\cdot):\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{R}
by minimizing the projected error
\mathbb{E}_{YW}[(Y-\mathbb{E}[h_{\theta_{a}}(A)\mid W])^{2}]
, where the conditional expectation
\mathbb{E}[h_{\theta_{a}}(A)\mid W]
is learned in stage I via another regression and parameterized by another neural network
h_{\theta_{w}}(\cdot):\mathcal{W}\to\mathbb{R}
.

Specifically,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \text{Stage I:}\qquad\qquad\theta_{w}^{\ast}(\theta_{a})&=\underset{\theta_{w}}{\arg\min}\quad\frac{1}{2m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left[\left(h_{\theta_{w}}(\mathbf{w}_{i})-h_{\theta_{a}}(\mathbf{a}_{i})\right)^{2}\right],\\
\text{Stage II:}\qquad\qquad\theta_{a}^{\ast}&=\underset{\theta_{a}}{\arg\min}\quad\frac{1}{2n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[\left(h_{\theta_{w}^{\ast}(\theta_{a})}(\mathbf{w}_{i})-\mathbf{y}_{i}\right)^{2}\right].\end{aligned}

(5)

The structural function
h_{\circ}
is estimated by the neural network
h_{\theta_{a}^{\ast}}
.
It is shown in
37
that if both Stage I and Stage II optimization algorithms reach their respective
global optima
, then the generalization error
\|h_{\theta_{a}^{\ast}}-h_{\circ}\|_{L^{2}(P_{A})}
achieves the minimax optimal rate, provided that the structural function
h_{\circ}
lies in a Besov space and the size of the neural networks increases as the number of samples increases.
Unfortunately, it remains a challenging and open problem whether this
global optimum
can be actually achieved.

To better illustrate the challenge of finding the global optimum of Eq. (
5
), we compare it against the fixed–feature 2SLS approach described in Eq. (
3
)–Eq. (
4
).
The two stages of fixed–feature 2SLS are sequential but
decoupled
. Stage I estimates the conditional mean embedding operator
V
without reference to the Stage II parameter
u
.
Once
\hat{V}
is obtained, Stage II simply solves a standard ridge regression problem for
u
.
By contrast, the DFIV formulation in Eq. (
5
) intertwines the two stages. The Stage I problem depends on the Stage II parameter
\theta_{a}
, and consequently Stage II optimization would require differentiation through the mapping
\theta_{a}\mapsto\theta_{w}^{\ast}(\theta_{a})
.
As a result, optimization no longer decomposes into two convex subproblems but instead takes the form of a more challenging
bilevel optimization
.
In addition to the bilevel structure, another challenge arises from the non-convexity in terms of the neural network parameters.

In this paper, to tackle the first challenge, we adopt the penalty gradient methods reformulating the bilevel optimization as a constrained optimization problem and then as a Lagrangian problem
(
67
;
41
)
; to tackle the second challenge, we follow the line of work on mean-field Langevin dynamics, which establishes global convergence of the training dynamics of two-layer neural networks
(
22
;
35
;
73
;
57
)
, which we review below.

3
Mean Field Langevin Dynamics (MFLD)

In this section, we briefly review the existing convergence results on gradient-based optimization of a two-layer neural network through the lens of
mean field Langevin dynamics
(MFLD).

Consider neural networks with a single hidden layer of size
N
:

h(\mathbf{a},\mathscr{X})=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Psi(\mathbf{a},x^{(i)})
where
\mathscr{X}=[x^{(1)},\ldots,x^{(N)}]\in(\mathbb{R}^{d_{a}})^{N}
are the network parameters and
\mathbf{a}
is the network input.
Here,
\Psi(\mathbf{a},x^{(i)})
denotes a neural network with a single-neuron, such as
\Psi(\mathbf{a},x)=\mathfrak{w}_{2}a(\mathfrak{w}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{a}+b)
with
x=(\mathfrak{w}_{1},\mathfrak{w}_{2},b)
and
a
being an activation function.
This representation offers a lifted perspective in which a two-layer neural network with fixed input
\mathbf{a}
is interpreted as a linear functional (i.e. expectation) on probability measures:
\mu\mapsto\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mu}[\Psi(\mathbf{a},X)]
where
\mu
is the empirical distribution
\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{x^{(i)}}
.
To emphasize the dependence on the network parameters
x
rather than the input
\mathbf{a}
, we adopt the notation
\Psi_{\mathbf{a}}(x):=\Psi(\mathbf{a},x)
.

Through lifting, the gradient-based optimization dynamics of the neural network parameters
\mathscr{X}=[x^{(1)},\ldots,x^{(N)}]
has been translated to the optimization dynamics of the probability measure
\mu
when quantized with
N
particles.
This connection has been pointed out
by
58
;
65
;
52
;
22
;
69
.
When the size of the hidden layer tends to infinity and the empirical distribution weakly converges to a probability measure,
\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{x^{(i)}}\to\mu
as
N\to\infty
, the resulting model is referred to as the
mean-field
limit of the neural network.

A key advantage of this lifted view is that the risk objective with
\ell_{2}
-norm regularization,

\displaystyle F(\mu):=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{y})\sim\rho}\left[(\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mu}[\Psi(\mathbf{a},X)]-\mathbf{y})^{2}\right]+\frac{\zeta}{2}\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mu}[\|X\|^{2}],

(6)

where
\rho
denotes a joint distribution over observations
(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{y})
, either empirical or population, becomes
linear convex
1
1

1

This is distinct from
geodesic
convexity. Linear convexity as in Eq. (
7
) means convexity along mixture curves:
\mu_{\vartheta}=\vartheta\mu+(1-\vartheta)\nu
.
In contrast, geodesic convexity refers to convexity along Wasserstein geodesics, where the interpolation
\mu_{\vartheta}
is obtained by optimal transport displacement.
in
\mu
.
The
\ell_{2}
regularization is crucial here to ensure the optimization dynamics would converge to a distribution that satisfies a Log-Sobolev inequality
(
3
)
.
Since the convergence analysis applies to any data distribution
\rho
, we do not distinguish between the empirical data and population data distributions here.
Therefore, for any probability measures
\mu,\nu
,

\displaystyle F(\vartheta\mu+(1-\vartheta)\nu)\leq\vartheta F(\mu)+(1-\vartheta)F(\nu),\quad\forall\vartheta\in(0,1).

(7)

As a result of such convexity, the corresponding gradient flow of
F
in the metric space
(\mathcal{P}_{2},W_{2})
: the space of probability measures on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
(with finite second moment) endowed with the Wasserstein-2 distance, has been proved to converge to its unique global minima
(
22
;
65
;
69
)
. Such global convergence indicates that gradient based training of two-layer neural networks can indeed find its global optimum in the mean field limit (
N\to\infty
).

Recent advances have strengthened this picture by establishing fast non-asymptotic convergence rates even with finite
N
, albeit under additional Gaussian noise
(
35
;
73
;
23
;
57
)
.
Such dynamics are known as the
mean field Langevin dynamics
:
for
\sigma>0
and an initial distribution
\mu_{0}
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}x_{t}=-\boldsymbol{\nabla}F(\mu)(x_{t})\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{2\sigma}\mathrm{~d}W_{t},\quad\mu_{t}=\operatorname{Law}(x_{t}).

(MFLD)

Here,
W_{t}
denotes the Brownian motion on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\boldsymbol{\nabla}F(\mu):\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
denotes the
Wasserstein gradient
of
F
at
\mu
, which is an element in the tangent space of
\mu
with respect to the Riemmanian geometry
(\mathcal{P}_{2},W_{2})
in the sense of the Otto’s calculus
(
79
)
.
Fortunately, for the set of functionals of the form in Eq. (
6
) that we primarily focus on in this paper, its Wasserstein gradient equals the Euclidean gradient of the first variation of
F
(defined in
1
), i.e.
\boldsymbol{\nabla}F(\mu)=\nabla[\delta F(\mu)]

(
1
, Lemma 10.4.1)
:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nabla}F(\mu):\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d},\quad\boldsymbol{\nabla}F(\mu)(x)=\mathbb{E}_{(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{y})\sim\rho}\big[(\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mu}[\Psi_{\mathbf{a}}(X)]-\mathbf{y})\nabla\Psi_{\mathbf{a}}(x)\big]+\zeta x.

Definition 1

(First variation)
.

The first variation
\delta G
of a functional
G:\mathcal{P}_{2}\to\mathbb{R}
at
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
is defined as a continuous functional
\mathcal{P}_{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
that satisfies
\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\epsilon^{-1}G(\epsilon\nu+(1-\epsilon)\mu)=\int\delta G(\mu)(x)\mathrm{d}(\nu-\mu)
for any
\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

The above (
MFLD
) can also be interpreted as the gradient flow of the functional with
entropy regularization
:
\mathscr{F}(\mu)=F(\mu)+\sigma\mathrm{Ent}(\mu)
in the Wasserstein geometry, since the gradient of the entropy functional
\mathrm{Ent}(\mu)
corresponds to a diffusion term in the Fokker–Planck equation, which yields an additive Brownian noise in the corresponding stochastic process
(
66
, Theorem 5.4)
.
Since
\mathrm{Ent}(\mu)
is strictly linear convex
(
24
, Theorem 2.7.3)
, one can immediately see that
\mathscr{F}
is also a
strictly linear convex
objective over
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{a}})
.

The ideal dynamics in (
MFLD
), however, cannot be simulated in practice due to the continuous time dynamics and infinite number of samples (i.e., mean field limit of two-layer neural network).
Therefore, one may consider the following implementable version of the MFLD with space- and time-discretization.
For initial particles
\mathscr{X}_{0}=[x_{0}^{(1)},\ldots,x_{0}^{(N)}]
and
s\in\{0,\ldots,S\}
for any
S\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
,

\displaystyle x_{s+1}^{(i)}=x_{s}^{(i)}-\gamma\boldsymbol{\nabla}F(\mu_{\mathscr{X},s})(x_{s}^{(i)})+\sqrt{2\sigma\gamma}\;\xi_{s}^{(i)},\quad\text{ where }\mu_{\mathscr{X},s}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{x_{s}^{(i)}},

(8)

for
i=1,\ldots,N
.
Here,
\{\xi_{s}^{(i)}\}_{i=1}^{N}
are
N
i.i.d. standard Gaussian random variables on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\gamma>0
is the step size. Substituting the explicit form of the Wasserstein gradient
\boldsymbol{\nabla}F
into Eq. (
8
), one sees that the resulting dynamics
coincide with
the training dynamics of two-layer neural networks
h(\mathbf{a},\mathscr{X})=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Psi(\mathbf{a},x^{(i)})
under Euclidean gradient descent with additional Gaussian noise.
Denote the unique global minimizer

\mu^{\ast}=\arg\min_{\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\mathscr{F}(\mu)
.
Therefore, a natural question that arises in the field of MFLD would be:
What is the convergence rate of
\mu_{\mathscr{X},s}
to
\mu^{\ast}
in terms of particle number
N
and iteration number
S
?

Over the years,
35
;
73
;
60
;
57
have presented an increasingly well-refined theoretical analysis of the above question, under mild regularity conditions that
\Psi
is smooth and bounded, which is satisfied by smooth activations like
\mathrm{tanh}
, sigmoid plus a smooth clipping on the neural network output
(
73
;
35
)
).
Among them, the state-of-the-art convergence results have been recently proved by
57
which enjoys the mildest dependence on the number of particles
N
, the number of iterations
S
and the dimension of the input
d
.
Here, we briefly review this result with the introduction of the following definitions.

Definition 2

(Logarithmic Sobolev inequality
(
3
, Definition 5.1.1)
)
.

For
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we say
\mu
satisfies the
logarithmic Sobolev inequality
(LSI) with constant
C>0
if for any locally Lipschitz function
g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
with
\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[g^{2}]<\infty
, we have

\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[g^{2}\log(g^{2})]-\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[g^{2}]\log(\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[g^{2}])\leq 2C^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\|\nabla g\|_{2}^{2}]
.

It is proved in Lemma 5 of
73
and
21
that the optimum
\mu^{\ast}
satisfies a LSI inequality with a LSI constant
C_{\mathrm{LSI}}=\Theta(\sigma^{-1}\exp(-\zeta^{-1}\sigma^{-1}\sqrt{d}))
. This constant deteriorates exponentially as the dimension
d
increases, as
\sigma\to 0
and as
\zeta\to 0
.

Remark 3.1
.

Two direct consequences of a probability distribution
\mu
satisfying LSI are that: for any probability distribution
\nu
,
\mathrm{KL}(\nu,\mu)\leq(2C_{\mathrm{LSI}})^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\nu}[\|\nabla\log(\frac{\mathrm{d}\nu}{\mathrm{d}\mu}(x))\|^{2}]=(2C_{\mathrm{LSI}})^{-1}\mathrm{FI}(\nu,\mu)
and
W_{2}^{2}(\nu,\mu)\leq 2C_{\mathrm{LSI}}^{-1}\mathrm{KL}(\nu,\mu)
.

Definition 3

(Bregman divergence)
.

For
\mu,\mu^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the Bregman divergence of a functional
F:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
is defined as

B_{F}(\mu,\mu^{\prime})=F(\mu)-F(\mu^{\prime})-\smallint\delta F(\mu^{\prime})\;\mathrm{d}(\mu-\mu^{\prime})
.

Remark 3.2
.

If
F_{0}
is a linear in terms of
\mu
, e.g.
F_{0}(\mu)=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[f]
, its Bregman divergence
B_{F_{0}}\equiv 0
. Thus, the Bregman divergence quantifies the deviation of
F
from its linear (first-order) approximation at
\mu^{\prime}
.
For a linear convex functional
F
, it is immediate that
B_{F}(\mu,\mu^{\prime})\geq 0
for all
\mu,\mu^{\prime}
.

To present the convergence result of
\mu_{\mathscr{X},s}
to
\mu^{\ast}
, we need to
define the following auxiliary objective
\mathscr{F}^{(N)}:\mathcal{P}_{2}((\mathbb{R}^{d})^{N})\to\mathbb{R}
and its corresponding global minimum
\mu_{\ast}^{(N)}
:

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}^{(N)}(\mu^{(N)})=N\mathbb{E}_{\mathscr{X}\sim\mu^{(N)}}[F(\mu_{\mathscr{X}})]+\sigma\mathrm{Ent}(\mu^{(N)}),\quad\mu_{\ast}^{(N)}=\arg\min_{\mu^{(N)}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}((\mathbb{R}^{d})^{N})}\mathscr{F}^{(N)}(\mu^{(N)}).

(9)

One can easily verify that if
\mu^{(N)}=\mu^{\otimes N}
is a
N
-fold product measure of
\mu
, then
\mathscr{F}^{(N)}(\mu^{(N)})\geq N\mathscr{F}(\mu)
by the linear convexity of
F
.
It is proved in Lemma 1 of
57
that for more general
\mu^{(N)}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}((\mathbb{R}^{d})^{N})
,

\displaystyle N^{-1}\mathscr{F}^{(N)}(\mu^{(N)})-\mathscr{F}(\mu_{*})

\displaystyle=N^{-1}\sigma\mathrm{KL}(\mu^{(N)},\mu_{*}^{\otimes N})+\mathbb{E}_{\mathscr{X}\sim\mu^{(N)}}[B_{F}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}},\mu_{*})]

(10)

\displaystyle N^{-1}\mathscr{F}^{(N)}(\mu^{(N)})-\mathscr{F}(\mu_{*})

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}(N^{-1})+(2C_{\mathrm{LSI}}N)^{-1}\sigma\mathrm{FI}(\mu^{(N)},\mu_{\ast}^{(N)}).

(11)

The first equality Eq. (
10
) indicates that
N^{-1}\mathscr{F}^{(N)}(\mu^{(N)})-\mathscr{F}(\mu_{*})
is a viable upper bound on
N^{-1}\sigma\mathrm{KL}(\mu^{(N)},\mu_{*}^{\otimes N})
, thanks to the non-negativity of the Bregman divergence.
Denote as
\mu_{s}^{(N)}
the joint distribution of the
N
particles
\mathscr{X}_{s}=[x_{s}^{(1)},\ldots,x_{s}^{(N)}]
of Eq. (
8
) at iteration
s\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
. To analyze the convergence of the empirical law
\mu_{\mathscr{X},s}
to
\mu^{\ast}
, it therefore suffices to study the decay of
N^{-1}\mathscr{F}^{(N)}(\mu^{(N)})-\mathscr{F}(\mu_{*})
to
0
.
The second inequality Eq. (
11
) is referred to as a
defective uniform logarithmic Sobolev inequality
in the MFLD literature.
Conceptually, Eq. (
11
) plays the role of a Polyak–Łojasiewicz (PL) inequality: the Fisher divergence
\mathrm{FI}(\mu^{(N)},\mu_{\ast}^{(N)})
measures the
L^{2}(\mu^{(N)})
norm of the update direction and upper bounds the distance between the current iterate and the global minimum.
The terminology “LSI” arises because, chaining Eq. (
10
) and Eq. (
11
), the Fisher divergence upper bounds KL divergence up to
\mathcal{O}(N^{-1})
and Bregman divergence.

Remark 3.3
.

The above two inequalities Eq. (
10
) and Eq. (
11
) are the key ingredients for establishing convergence of MFLD, which will later be proved in the context of our bilevel optimization algorithm
F
2
BMLD
:
5.9
corresponds to Eq. (
10
) and
5.10
corresponds to Eq. (
11
).

With the above two key inequalities Eq. (
10
) and Eq. (
11
), we are now ready to present the convergence
(
57
, Theorem 1)
.
For any number of iterations
S\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
,

\displaystyle N^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathscr{F}^{(N)}(\mu_{S}^{(N)})\right]-\mathscr{F}(\mu_{*})\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{N}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\gamma^{2}+\gamma\sigma d}{C_{\mathrm{LSI}}\sigma}\right)+\exp(-\gamma C_{\mathrm{LSI}}\sigma S)\Delta_{0}^{(N)}.

(12)

Here, the expectation is taken with respect to the randonmess in the initial particles
[x_{0}^{(1)},\ldots,x_{0}^{(N)}]\sim\mu_{0}^{(N)}
and the Gaussian noise at each iteration.

\mathcal{O}(N^{-1})
represents the particle approximation error and
\mathcal{O}(\frac{\gamma^{2}+\gamma\sigma d}{C_{\mathrm{LSI}}\sigma})
represents the time-discretization error.
The term
\Delta_{0}^{(N)}:=N^{-1}\mathbb{E}[\mathscr{F}^{(N)}(\mu_{0}^{(N)})]-\mathscr{F}(\mu_{\ast})
denotes the initial error, which decays exponentially fast in terms of
S
as a consequence of the PL (log-Sobolev) inequality.

The above upper bound on
N^{-1}\mathbb{E}[\mathscr{F}^{(N)}(\mu_{S}^{(N)})]-\mathscr{F}(\mu_{*})
can be translated to upper bound on KL divergence through Eq. (
10
): for any number of iterations
S\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
,

\displaystyle N^{-1}\sigma\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{KL}(\mu_{S}^{(N)},\mu_{\ast}^{\otimes N})]\leq N^{-1}\mathbb{E}[\mathscr{F}^{(N)}(\mu_{S}^{(N)})]-\mathscr{F}(\mu_{*}).

(13)

The above equation suggests a phenomenon known as the
propagation of chaos
that the particles become asymptotically independent as both
N,S
tend to infinity
(
74
)
. The above upper bound on KL divergence implies convergence of neural network output
(
57
, Proposition 1)
. Define
\hat{h}_{S}(\mathbf{a})=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Psi_{\mathbf{a}}(x_{S}^{(i)})
the output of a trained neural network where the particles
\{x_{S}^{(i)}\}_{i=1}^{N}
follow a joint distribution
\mu_{S}^{(N)}
, and define
h_{\ast}(\mathbf{a})=\int\Psi_{\mathbf{a}}(x)\mathrm{d}\mu_{\ast}(x)
the output of the optimal mean-field neural network. For any
\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{A}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\hat{h}_{S}(\mathbf{a})-h_{\ast}(\mathbf{a})\right)^{2}\right]\leq\sqrt{\frac{1}{N}\mathrm{KL}(\mu_{S}^{(N)},\mu_{\ast}^{\otimes N})}+\mathcal{O}(N^{-1}).

4
Bilevel Mean Field Langevin Dynamics

In this section, motivated by mean field Langevin dynamics, we first present in
Section

4.1
a lifted perspective of the bilevel optimization problem in Eq. (
5
). Through this lifted perspective, our aim is to establish convergence to the
global optimum
, thereby completing the optimization theory of neural networks in 2SLS for NPIV regression, as emphasized in the introduction.

Solving this lifted problem is challenging, however, as it amounts to a bilevel optimization in the space of probability measures.
The two standard approaches in bilevel optimization—implicit gradient methods
(
31
)
and explicit gradient methods
(
29
;
50
)
—are ineffective in this setting: the former requires second-order derivatives on
\mathcal{P}
, which are difficult to compute in practice (see
Section

A.2
), while the latter relies on automatic differentiation, which does not extend naturally to probability measures.
To overcome this difficulty, we take inspiration from recent advances in bilevel optimization
(
67
;
41
)
, which reformulate the stage-I problem as a Lagrangian penalty embedded in stage II.
This reformulation requires only first-order gradients that can naturally extend to Wasserstein gradients in the space of probability measures.
Building on this idea, we introduce
F
2
BMLD
in
Section

4.2
, a
fully first-order
algorithm for solving Eq. (
5
), thereby avoiding higher-order derivatives.
Its convergence will be established in
Section

5
.

4.1
Mean field formulation of DFIV

Consider two-layer neural networks with a single hidden layer:
h(\mathbf{a},\mathscr{X})=\frac{1}{N_{x}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{x}}\Psi_{\mathbf{a}}(x^{(i)})
(resp.
h(\mathbf{w},\mathscr{Z})=\frac{1}{N_{z}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{z}}\Psi_{\mathbf{w}}(z^{(i)})
) where
\mathscr{X}=[x^{(1)},\ldots,x^{(N_{x})}]\in(\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}})^{N_{x}}
(resp.
\mathscr{Z}=[z^{(1)},\ldots,z^{(N_{z})}]\in(\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}})^{N_{z}}
) are the network parameters and
\mathbf{a}
(resp.
\mathbf{w}
) is the network input.
Therefore, the bilevel optimization problem in Eq. (
5
) can be re-written as the following:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \text{Stage I:}\qquad\qquad\mathscr{Z}^{\ast}(\mathscr{X})&=\underset{\mathscr{Z}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}})^{N_{x}}}{\arg\min}\quad\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\left(h(\mathbf{w},\mathscr{Z})-h(\mathbf{a},\mathscr{X})\right)^{2}\right],\\
\text{Stage II:}\qquad\qquad\qquad\mathscr{X}^{\ast}&=\underset{\mathscr{X}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}})^{N_{z}}}{\arg\min}\quad\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\left(h(\mathbf{w},\mathscr{Z}^{\ast}(\mathscr{X}))-\mathbf{y}\right)^{2}\right].\end{aligned}

(14)

We use
\mathbb{E}_{\rho}
to denote expectation with respect to a generic joint data distribution
\rho
over
(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{w})\in\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{W}
.
In this section, we do not distinguish between the population and the empirical distribution, since this distinction is irrelevant for analyzing convergence of the optimization dynamics.
In contrast, when studying generalization of the learned network in
Section

6
, we will explicitly take
\rho
to be the empirical distribution consisting of finite i.i.d. samples from the data generating distribution
P
.

Inspired by MFLD, we adopt a
lifted perspective
of both neural networks
\int\Psi_{\mathbf{a}}(x)\mathrm{d}\mu_{x}(x)
and
\int\Psi_{\mathbf{w}}(z)\mathrm{d}\mu_{z}(z)
where
\mu_{x},\mu_{z}
are the mean-field limit of the hidden layer. Under
\ell_{2}
and entropic regularizations, we obtain the following bilevel optimization problem over
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}})
and
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}})
.

\displaystyle\text{Stage I:}\quad\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})=\underset{\mu_{z}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}})}{\arg\min}\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[(\smallint\!_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}}}\Psi_{\mathbf{w}}\mathrm{d}\mu_{z}-\smallint\!_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}}}\Psi_{\mathbf{a}}\mathrm{d}\mu_{x})^{2}]+\frac{\zeta_{1}}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{z}}[\|z\|^{2}]+\sigma_{1}\mathrm{Ent}(\mu_{z}),

\displaystyle\text{Stage II:}\quad\mu_{x}^{\ast}=\underset{\mu_{x}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}})}{\arg\min}\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[(\smallint\!_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}}}\Psi_{\mathbf{w}}\mathrm{d}\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})-\mathbf{y})^{2}]+\frac{\zeta_{2}}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{x}}[\|x\|^{2}]+\sigma_{2}\mathrm{Ent}(\mu_{x}).

(Bi-MFLD)

Here,
\sigma_{1},\zeta_{1},\sigma_{2},\zeta_{2}>0
are levels of
\ell_{2}
and entropic regularization in Stage I and Stage II, respectively.
The stage I solution approximates the conditional expectation
\int\Psi_{\mathbf{w}}(z)\mathrm{d}\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})\approx\mathbb{E}[\int\Psi_{A}(x)\mathrm{d}\mu_{x}\mid W=\mathbf{w}]
; and the stage II solution approximates the structural function
\int\Psi_{\mathbf{a}}(x)\mathrm{d}\mu_{x}^{\ast}\approx h_{\circ}(\mathbf{a})
.

To help with the analysis, we denote the following objectives

\displaystyle U_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[(\smallint\Psi_{\mathbf{w}}(z)\;\mathrm{d}\mu_{z}(z)-\smallint\Psi_{\mathbf{a}}(x)\;\mathrm{d}\mu_{x}(x))^{2}],

\displaystyle\quad U_{2}(\mu_{z})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}[(\smallint\Psi_{\mathbf{w}}(z)\;\mathrm{d}\mu_{z}(z)-\mathbf{y})^{2}],

which are mean squared error of both stages without any regularization.
We also denote

\displaystyle F_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})=U_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})+\frac{\zeta_{1}}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{z}}[\|z\|^{2}],\quad\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})=F_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})+\sigma_{1}\mathrm{Ent}(\mu_{z})

\displaystyle F_{2}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})=U_{2}(\mu_{z})+\frac{\zeta_{2}}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{x}}[\|x\|^{2}],\quad\mathscr{F}_{2}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})=F_{2}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})+\sigma_{2}\mathrm{Ent}(\mu_{x}),

which are objectives of both stages with
\ell_{2}
and entropic regularization.
Following the terminology of bilevel optimization, we sometimes refer to stage I as ‘inner-loop’ optimization and stage II as ‘outer-loop’ optimization.
For the entropy to be finite, both stage I and stage II solutions must be absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure. Hence, we sometimes abuse
\mu(x)
to denote both the probability measure and its density.

Throughout the following sections, we make the following assumptions.

Assumption 1

(Bounded target)
.

There exists a universal constant
M
such that the target random variable
|Y|\leq M
and
|h_{\circ}(X)|\leq M
almost surely.

1
can be relaxed. When
\rho
is the true data generating distribution
P
, it suffices to only assume
|h_{\circ}(\mathbf{a})|\leq M
for any
\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{A}
. When
\rho
is the empirical data distribution consisting of
n
i.i.d. samples from
P
, it suffices to assume bounded
h_{\circ}
and sub-Gaussian residual
Y-(Th_{\circ})(Z)
so that
\max_{i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}|\mathbf{y}_{i}|
is
\mathcal{O}(\log n)
with high probability
(
78
, Exercise 2.5.10)
.
This would result in an extra logarithmic factor in the final bound.

Assumption 2

(Bounded and smooth neural networks)
.

There exists a universal positive constant
R
such that
\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}},\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{A}}|\Psi_{\mathbf{a}}(x)|\leq R
and
\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}},\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{W}}|\Psi_{\mathbf{w}}(z)|\leq R
. Also,
\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}},\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{A}}|\nabla_{x}\Psi_{\mathbf{a}}(x)|\leq R
and
\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}},\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{W}}|\nabla_{z}\Psi_{\mathbf{w}}(z)|\leq R
.

2
is standard in the literature of mean field Langevin dynamics (e.g.
35
,
73
,
57
). It is satisfied for instance by neural networks of the form
\Psi_{\mathbf{a}}(x)=\mathfrak{w}_{2}a(\mathfrak{w}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{a})
for
x=(\mathfrak{w}_{1},\mathfrak{w}_{2})
with a smooth clipping and with smooth activation function
a
such as
\tanh
, sigmoid.

We begin by establishing that the solution to (
Bi-MFLD
) exists and is well-defined. To this end, it is necessary to verify several key properties of the problem: specifically, the partial convexity of
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
, as well as the continuity of the mapping
\mu_{x}\mapsto\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})
.

Proposition 4.1

(Partial convexity of
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
)
.

For any fixed
\mu_{x}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}})
, the mappings
\mu_{z}\mapsto F_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})
,
\mu_{z}\mapsto F_{2}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})
are
linear
convex. For any fixed
\mu_{z}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}})
, the mappings
\mu_{x}\mapsto F_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})
the mapping
\mu_{x}\mapsto F_{2}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})
are also
linear
convex.

Proof.

The proof is trivial since both
U_{1},U_{2}
are composition of a linear mapping
\mu\mapsto\int\Psi\mathrm{d}\mu
and a quadratic cost function, and since
\ell_{2}
regularizations
\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{x}}[\|x\|^{2}],\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{z}}[\|z\|^{2}]
are linear functionals.
∎

Proposition 4.2

(Continuity of mapping
\mu_{x}\mapsto\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})
)
.

Suppose
2
holds.
Let
\mu_{x},\mu_{x}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}})
. Let
\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})
and
\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}^{\prime})
be the solution to the Stage I optimization problem in (
Bi-MFLD
). Then, we have
\mathrm{KL}(\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}),\;\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}^{\prime}))+\mathrm{KL}(\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}^{\prime}),\;\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))\leq\frac{R^{2}}{8\sigma_{1}}\mathrm{KL}(\mu_{x},\mu_{x}^{\prime})
.

Proof.

This proposition follows as a special case of the more general result in
10.4
.
∎

Proposition 4.3

(Existence of solutions in (
Bi-MFLD
))
.

Suppose
1
and
2
hold. For any
\mu_{x}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}})
, the solution
\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})
to the Stage I optimization problem exists, is unique, is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure, and belongs to
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}})
. The solution
\mu_{x}^{\ast}
to the Stage II optimization problem exists, is not necessarily unique, is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure, and belongs to
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}})
.

The proof can be found in
Section

9.1.1
.

4.3
ensures that the solutions to the two optimization problems in (
Bi-MFLD
) exist and are well-defined. The proof of the first half of
4.3
is standard and follows exactly that of Proposition 2.5 in
35
; whereas the proof of the second half is novel and relies on the continuity of the mapping
\mu_{x}\mapsto F_{2}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))
in terms of the weak topology. Unfortunately, the solution to the outer loop might not be unique due to the lack of convexity of the nested mapping
\mu_{x}\mapsto\mathscr{F}_{2}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))
.

Note that in the original formulation of DFIV in
85
and
37
, the entropic regularizations
\sigma_{1}\mathrm{Ent}(\mu_{x})
and
\sigma_{2}\mathrm{Ent}(\mu_{z})
are not present in the respective objectives.
As reviewed in
Section

3
, entropic regularizations are crucial for establishing fast, non-asymptotic finite-particle convergence of MFLD.
The following proposition establishes that the solutions of the entropically regularized (
Bi-MFLD
) remain consistent with those of the original formulation of DFIV in the limit
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\to 0
.

Proposition 4.4

(
\Gamma
-convergence as
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\to(0,0)
)
.

Suppose
1
and
2
hold.
Let
\mu_{z,\sigma_{1}}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})
be the solution to the inner-loop optimization problem in (
Bi-MFLD
) with entropic regularization scale
\sigma_{1}
.
We write the stage II objective as
\mathscr{F}_{2,(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z,\sigma_{1}}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))=U_{2}(\mu_{z,\sigma_{1}}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))+\frac{\zeta_{2}}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{x}}[\|x\|^{2}]+\sigma_{2}\mathrm{Ent}(\mu_{x})
with an explicit emphasis on its dependence on the entropic regularization scales
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}
.
Then, as
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\to(0,0)
, the family of functionals
\mathscr{F}_{2,(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})}
would
\Gamma
-converge to
\mathscr{F}_{2,(0,0)}
with respect to the weak topology on
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}})
.

The proof can be found in
Section

9.1.2
.
The
\Gamma
-convergence result above guarantees stability of minimizers under vanishing entropic regularization.
Let
(\mu^{\ast}_{x,\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}})
denote a sequence of global minimizers of
\mathscr{F}_{2,(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})}
introduced in
4.4
, i.e.,

\mu^{\ast}_{x,\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}=\arg\min_{\mu_{x}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}})}\mathscr{F}_{2,(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z,\sigma_{1}}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))
.
If this sequence converges (in the sense of weak topology) to some
{\mu}^{\diamond}_{x}
as
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\to(0,0)
, then
{\mu}^{\diamond}_{x}
is a global minimizer of
\mathscr{F}_{2,(0,0)}
.

4.2
Penalty gradient method

Having established the existence of solutions (
4.3
) and their consistency with the original DFIV problem as
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\to(0,0)
(
4.4
), we now introduce a
fully first-order
bilevel mean field Langevin dynamics for solving (
Bi-MFLD
), termed by
F
2
BMLD
.
Our algorithm builds on recent advances in bilevel optimization, where the inner-loop problem is reformulated as a constraint embedded in the outer-loop problem
(
67
;
41
)
.

In (
Bi-MFLD
), the inner-loop optimization corresponds to a standard mean-field Langevin dynamics, which enjoys fast convergence rates given the convexity of the mapping
\mu_{z}\mapsto F_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})
proved in
4.1
for any fixed
\mu_{x}
.
The primary challenge, however, lies in the outer-loop optimization, as the
nested
mapping
\mu_{x}\mapsto F_{2}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))
is no longer convex, and the Wasserstein gradient of this mapping requires higher-order gradients which are computationally expensive (see
A.4
).
To address this challenge, we adopt the reformulation which casts the bilevel optimization problem as the following constrained optimization problem:

\displaystyle\min_{\mu_{x},\mu_{z}}\mathscr{F}_{2}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z}),\quad\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})-\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))\leq\varepsilon.

(
\varepsilon
-constrained)

Here
\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})=\arg\min_{\mu_{z}}\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})
is the solution to the inner-loop optimization.
It is immediate that the constrained problem (

ε

-constrained
) recovers the original (
Bi-MFLD
) when
\varepsilon=0
.

The above constrained optimization is still challenging to solve due to the imposed hard constraints. Following
67
and
41
, we formulate the above constrained optimization problem as the following Lagrangian problem.

\displaystyle(\mu_{x,\lambda}^{\ast},\mu_{z,\lambda}^{\ast})=\arg\min_{\mu_{x},\mu_{z}}\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z}):=\mathscr{F}_{2}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})+\lambda\left(\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})-\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))\right).

(
\lambda
-penalty)

Here,
\lambda>0
is the Lagrange multiplier.
It is again immediate that the Lagrangian formulation (

λ

-penalty
) recovers the original (
Bi-MFLD
) when
\lambda=\infty
.
The estimator of the structural function
h_{\circ}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{R}
would be
\mathbf{a}\mapsto\int\Psi_{\mathbf{a}}(x)\mathrm{d}\mu_{x,\lambda}^{\ast}
.

Next, we establish a more quantitative connection between (

λ

-penalty
), (

ε

-constrained
) and the original (
Bi-MFLD
) in terms of the Lagrange multiplier
\lambda
.
In particular, we prove that one can recover the approximate global solution of (
Bi-MFLD
)
with a global solution of (

λ

-penalty
).
Before we introduce the result, we give the following definition of an
\epsilon
-global-minimum.

Definition 4

(
\epsilon
-global-minimum)
.

Given a functional
\ell:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\mapsto\mathbb{R}
, for the optimization problem defined as
\min_{\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\ell(\mu)
, we say
\mu_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is an
\epsilon
-global-minimum of this problem if
\ell(\mu_{0})\leq\ell(\mu)+\epsilon
for any
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Recall that
\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\cdot)
and
\mu_{x}^{\ast}
are the global optimal solution of the inner-loop and the outer-loop in (
Bi-MFLD
).
Define
(\mu_{x,\lambda}^{(\epsilon)},\mu_{z,\lambda}^{(\epsilon)})
as the
\epsilon
-global-minimum of (

λ

-penalty
).
When
\epsilon=0
, it becomes the true global minimum
(\mu_{x,\lambda}^{(0)},\mu_{z,\lambda}^{(0)})=(\mu_{x,\lambda}^{\ast},\mu_{z,\lambda}^{\ast})=\arg\min_{\mu_{x},\mu_{z}}\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})
.

Theorem 4.5

(Relations of solutions for (
Bi-MFLD
), (

ε

-constrained
) and (

λ

-penalty
))
.

Suppose
1
and
2
hold. Then, we have the following relations:

1.

The global solution of (
Bi-MFLD
) is
\frac{R^{2}(R+M)^{2}}{8\lambda\sigma_{1}}
-global-minimum of (

λ

-penalty
).

2.

Given
\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}>0
and
\lambda_{0}=\epsilon_{1}^{-1}\frac{R^{2}(R+M)^{2}}{8\sigma_{1}}
, let
(\mu_{x,\lambda}^{(\epsilon_{2})},\mu_{z,\lambda}^{(\epsilon_{2})})
be
\epsilon_{2}
-global-minimum of (

λ

-penalty
) with
\lambda>\lambda_{0}
. Then,
(\mu_{x,\lambda}^{(\epsilon_{2})},\mu_{z,\lambda}^{(\epsilon_{2})})
is also
\epsilon_{2}
-global-minimum of (

ε

-constrained
) with
\varepsilon\leq(\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{2})/(\lambda-\lambda_{0})
.

3.

Let
(\mu_{x,\varepsilon}^{(\epsilon_{3})},\mu_{z,\varepsilon}^{(\epsilon_{3})})
be
\epsilon_{3}
-global-minimum of (

ε

-constrained
). Then,
\mathscr{F}_{2}(\mu_{x,\varepsilon}^{(\epsilon_{3})},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x,\varepsilon}^{(\epsilon_{3})}))-R(R+M)\sqrt{(2\sigma_{1})^{-1}\varepsilon}\leq\mathscr{F}_{2}(\mu_{x,\varepsilon}^{(\epsilon_{3})},\mu_{z,\varepsilon}^{(\epsilon_{3})})\leq\mathscr{F}_{2}(\mu_{x}^{\ast},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}^{\ast}))+\epsilon_{3}
.

The proof can be found in
Section

9.1.3
.

Remark 4.6
.

From the second and the third bullet points of the above proposition, we can see that the global solution to the Lagrangian problem
\arg\min_{\mu_{x},\mu_{z}}\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})
can be a good approximation of the solution to the original bilevel optimization problem in (
Bi-MFLD
) for
\lambda>\lambda_{0}
. This relationship will be crucial in the generalization analysis in
Section

6
.

Next, we propose a concrete algorithm to solve (

λ

-penalty
).
A particular advantage of the Lagrangian formulation is that the Wasserstein gradient of the Lagrangian objective
\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}
only involves first-order derivatives, as shown in the following proposition.

Proposition 4.7

(Wasserstein gradient of
\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}
)
.

Let
\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})=\arg\min_{\mu_{z}}\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})
be the solution to the inner-loop optimization. Then, for
\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}
defined in (

λ

-penalty
),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{1}\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{1}\mathscr{F}_{2}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})+\lambda\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{1}\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})-\lambda\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{1}\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{2}\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{2}\mathscr{F}_{2}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})+\lambda\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{2}\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z}).

\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{1}
(resp.
\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{2}
) denotes the Wasserstein gradient with respect to the first (resp. second) argument.

Proof.

The gradient of
\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}
with respect to
\mu_{x}
requires taking the gradient of the nested mapping
\mu_{x}\mapsto\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))
. Fortunately, by the envelope theorem, the optimality of
\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})
ensures that the Wasserstein gradient depends solely on the first argument of
\mathscr{F}_{1}
, and no additional terms arise from the dependence of
\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})
on
\mu_{x}
.
The derivative of
\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}
with respect to
\mu_{z}
is standard as it does not involve nested mapping.
∎

To aid the following analysis in the spirit of MFLD, we define another objective
L_{\lambda}
which is
\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}
yet excluding the entropic regularization on
\mu_{x}
.

\displaystyle L_{\lambda}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})=F_{2}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})+\lambda\left(\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})-\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))\right).

(15)

Instead of performing mean-field Langevin dynamics (MFLD) directly on
L_{\lambda}
, or equivalently, running Wasserstein gradient flow on
\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}
jointly with respect to
(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})
, we adopt a sequential optimization scheme. Specifically, we employ an
alternating strategy
: for a fixed
\mu_{x}
, we first optimize
\mu_{z}
to convergence, obtaining
\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})=\arg\min_{\mu_{z}}\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})
,
and subsequently we update
\mu_{x}
by performing another MFLD on the reduced objective
\mu_{x}\mapsto L_{\lambda}(\mu_{x},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))
. This approach is motivated by the structural properties of
L_{\lambda}
(and, analogously,
\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}
), which exhibits
partial convexity
—convexity in
\mu_{z}
for fixed
\mu_{x}
—but not
joint convexity
in
(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})
.
The lack of joint convexity is easy to verify: even a simple mapping such as
(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})\mapsto(\int\Psi(x)\mathrm{d}\mu_{x}(x)-\int\Psi(z)\mathrm{d}\mu_{z}(z))^{2}
is not convex due to subtraction.
The partial convexity is formalized in the following proposition.

Proposition 4.8

(Convexity of
\mu_{z}\mapsto L_{\lambda}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})
)
.

The mapping
\mu_{z}\mapsto L_{\lambda}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})
is
linear
convex, for any fixed
\mu_{x}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}})
.

Proof.

The proof is straightforward from
4.1
.
∎

Algorithm 1

InnerLoop
(
\mu_{x}
,
T
,
\alpha
,
\beta
,
\lambda
,
\sigma_{1}
)

Input:
Inner-loop iteration count
T
, step sizes
\alpha,\beta
, penalty parameter
\lambda
, diffusion noise level
\sigma_{1}
.

Initialize
\mu_{\mathscr{Z},0}=\frac{1}{N_{z}}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{z}}\delta_{z_{0}^{(j)}}
.

for

t=0,\ldots,T

do

for

i=1,\ldots,N_{z}

do

z_{t+1}^{(i)}=z_{t}^{(i)}-\alpha\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{2}F_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{\mathscr{Z},t})(z_{t}^{(i)})+\sqrt{2\alpha\sigma_{1}}\xi_{z,t}^{(i)},\quad\xi_{z,t}^{(i)}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathrm{Id}_{d_{z}})
.

end

for

Update
\mu_{\mathscr{Z},t}=\frac{1}{N_{z}}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{z}}\delta_{z_{t}^{(j)}}
.

end

for

Initialize
\tilde{\mu}_{\mathscr{Z},0}=\frac{1}{N_{z}}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{z}}\delta_{\tilde{z}_{0}^{(j)}}
.

for

t=0,\ldots,T

do

for

i=1,\ldots,N_{z}

do

\begin{aligned} \tilde{z}_{t+1}^{(i)}=\tilde{z}_{t}^{(i)}-\beta\boldsymbol{\nabla}U_{2}(\tilde{\mu}_{\mathscr{Z},t})(\tilde{z}_{t}^{(i)})-\beta\lambda\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{2}F_{1}(\mu_{x},\tilde{\mu}_{\mathscr{Z},t})(\tilde{z}_{t}^{(i)})+\sqrt{2\beta\lambda\sigma_{1}}\tilde{\xi}_{z,t}^{(i)},\text{ }\tilde{\xi}_{z,t}^{(i)}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathrm{Id}_{d_{z}})\end{aligned}
.

end

for

Update
\tilde{\mu}_{\mathscr{Z},t}=\frac{1}{N_{z}}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{z}}\delta_{\tilde{z}_{t}^{(j)}}
.

end

for

Return:
\mu_{\mathscr{Z},T}(\mu_{x}),\tilde{\mu}_{\mathscr{Z},T}(\mu_{x})
.

Remark 4.9
.

In the inner loop, we solve the following two optimization problems:

\displaystyle\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})=\arg\min_{\mu_{z}}\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})=\arg\min_{\mu_{z}}F_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})+\sigma_{1}\mathrm{Ent}(\mu_{z}),

(16)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})=\arg\min_{\mu_{z}}\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})=\arg\min_{\mu_{z}}F_{2}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})+\lambda F_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})+\lambda\sigma_{1}\mathrm{Ent}(\mu_{z}).

(17)

\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})
and
\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})
are two distinct quantities.

\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})
is the solution to the stage I optimization problem in (
Bi-MFLD
), and it approximates the conditional expectation operator
T
in the sense that
\int\Psi_{\mathbf{w}}(z)\mathrm{d}\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})\approx T(\int\Psi_{A}(x)\mathrm{d}\mu_{x})(\mathbf{w})
.
In contrast,
\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})
is the partial solution to the Lagrangian problem in (

λ

-penalty
)
2
2

2

Although
\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})
in Eq. (
17
) depends on the Lagrange multiplier
\lambda
, we do not make this dependence explicit in the notation for two reasons: (i) to keep the notation lightweight, and (ii) to retain notation symmetric with
{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})
in Eq. (
16
) which is also computed in the inner loop.
. Unlike
\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})
,
\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})
is only an intermediate quantity and does not admit a direct interpretation in the NPIV problem of Eq. (
1
).
The motivation for computing the partial solution of (

λ

-penalty
) comes from the partial convexity of the mapping
\mu_{z}\mapsto\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})
, as established in
4.8
.
Moreover, since both optimization problems above are taken with respect to
\mu_{z}
while keeping
\mu_{x}
fixed, this naturally suggests solving them within the same inner loop.

The precise inner-loop algorithm is presented in
Algorithm

1
, where both dynamics are simulated for
T
iterations and
N_{z}
particles using step sizes
\alpha
and
\beta
.
Owing to the convexity of both objectives
\mu_{x}\mapsto\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})
and
\mu_{x}\mapsto\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})
, the convergence of the corresponding mean-field Langevin dynamics is expected to be fast, following the same reasoning as in
Section

3
.

After obtaining both
\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})
and
\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})
, to complete the solution to the Lagrangian problem (

λ

-penalty
), what remains is to solve
\mu_{x,\lambda}^{\ast}=\arg\min_{\mu_{x}}\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}(\mu_{x},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))
.
Recall that
\mu_{x,\lambda}^{\ast}
is the main quantity of interest as
\mathbf{a}\mapsto\int\Psi_{\mathbf{a}}(x)\mathrm{d}\mu_{x,\lambda}^{\ast}
is our final estimator of the structural function
h_{\circ}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{R}
.
Therefore, for the outer loop, the target is to find the following:

\displaystyle\mu_{x,\lambda}^{\ast}

\displaystyle=\arg\min_{\mu_{x}}\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}(\mu_{x},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))=\arg\min_{\mu_{x}}L_{\lambda}(\mu_{x},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))+\sigma_{2}\mathrm{Ent}(\mu_{x})

\displaystyle=\arg\min_{\mu_{x}}F_{2}(\mu_{x},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))+\lambda(\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{x},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))-\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})))+\sigma_{2}\mathrm{Ent}(\mu_{x}).

From the optimality of
\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})
and
\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})
, along with the envelope theorem, the Wasserstein gradient of the mapping
\mu_{x}\mapsto L_{\lambda}(\mu_{x},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))
can be written as

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nabla}L_{\lambda}(\mu_{x},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))(x)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{1}L_{\lambda}(\mu_{x},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))(x)

\displaystyle\hskip-20.0pt=\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{1}F_{2}(\mu_{x},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))(x)+\lambda(\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{1}\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{x},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))(x)-\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{1}\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))(x))

\displaystyle\hskip-20.0pt=\zeta_{2}x+\lambda(\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{1}U_{1}(\mu_{x},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))(x)-\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{1}U_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))(x)).

(18)

Fortunately, the Wasserstein gradient above admits a closed-form expression and an efficient finite-particle implementation, because it does not involve the nested mappings
\mu_{z}\mapsto\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})
or
\mu_{z}\mapsto\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})
, and consequently, it coincides with the Euclidean gradient of its first variation.
The exact outer-loop algorithm is outlined in
Algorithm

2
, where
\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})
and
\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})
are replaced with the outputs of the inner-loop.
The output of
Algorithm

2

\mu_{\mathscr{X},S}=\frac{1}{N_{x}}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{x}}\delta_{x_{S}^{(j)}}
corresponds to the result of a time-discretized, finite-particle implementation of the mean field Langevin dynamics, simulated for
S
iterations and
N_{x}
particles using a positive step size
\gamma
.

Algorithm 2

\texttt{F${}^{2}$BMLD}:\textsc{Outerloop}(S,T,\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\lambda,\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})

Input:
Inner-loop iteration count
T
, outer-loop iteration count
S
, step sizes
\alpha,\beta,\gamma
, penalty parameter
\lambda
, diffusion noise level
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}
.

1:

Initialize
\mu_{\mathscr{X},0}=\frac{1}{N_{x}}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{x}}\delta_{x_{0}^{(j)}}
.

2:

for

s=0,\ldots,S

do

3:

\tilde{\mu}_{\mathscr{Z},s},\mu_{\mathscr{Z},s}\leftarrow\textsc{InnerLoop}(\mu_{\mathscr{X},s},T,\alpha,\beta,\lambda,\sigma_{1})
.

4:

for

i=1,\ldots,N_{x}

do

5:

\begin{aligned} x_{s+1}^{(i)}&=x_{s}^{(i)}-\gamma\left(\zeta_{2}x_{s}^{(i)}+\lambda(\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{1}U_{1}(\mu_{\mathscr{X},s},\tilde{\mu}_{\mathscr{Z},s})(x_{s}^{(i)})-\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{1}U_{1}(\mu_{\mathscr{X},s},\mu_{\mathscr{Z},s})(x_{s}^{(i)}))\right)\\
&\hskip 170.00026pt+\sqrt{2\gamma\sigma_{2}}\xi_{x,s}^{(i)},\quad\xi_{x,s}^{(i)}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathrm{Id}_{d_{x}}).\end{aligned}

6:

end

for

7:

Update
\mu_{\mathscr{X},s}=\frac{1}{N_{x}}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{x}}\delta_{x_{s}^{(j)}}
.

8:

end

for

Return:
\mu_{\mathscr{X},S}
.

Remark 4.10

(A fully first-order algorithm)
.

A key advantage of the proposed algorithm,
F
2
BMLD
, derived from the Lagrangian reformulation of the original bilevel optimization problem, is that it relies solely on first-order Wasserstein gradients, which after finite-particle implementation, would correspond to standard first-order Euclidean gradients, making the method computationally efficient.
In contrast, existing approaches that leverage deep neural networks to solve 2SLS require taking derivatives through the nested mappings, which are computationally demanding
(
85
)
.
The notable exception is the method proposed by
64
, which only requires
functional
second-order derivatives—quantities that often admit closed-form expressions for common objectives such as the mean squared loss. Their analysis remains a weight-space perspective, however, and therefore establishes convergence only to a stationary point.

Remark 4.11

(Implicit gradient method)
.

In
Section

A.2
, we show that directly solving (
Bi-MFLD
) without resorting to its Lagrangian formulation would lead to an optimization problem over the space of probability measures that is intractable with a finite number of particles. The underlying intuition is that, unlike variational integrals in Equation 10.4.1 of
1
, the mapping of the outer-level objective
\mu_{x}\mapsto\mathscr{F}_{2}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))
does not preserve a structure where the Wasserstein gradient can be expressed simply as the gradient of its first variation. Consequently, we must revert to the original definition of the Fréchet subdifferential, as introduced in Section 10 of
1
, which yields Wasserstein gradients that involve terms difficult to approximate with finitely many particles (see
A.4
for details), unlike our fully first order method
F
2
BMLD
.

5
Convergence of
F
2
BMLD

In this section, we establish the non-asymptotic convergence of the output
\mu_{\mathscr{X},s}
of the proposed
F
2
BMLD
towards the global optimum
\mu_{x,\lambda}^{*}
of the Lagrangian problem (

λ

-penalty
), for a fixed
\lambda>0
. We focus explicitly on
\mu_{x}
, rather than
\mu_{z}
, since
\mu_{x}
corresponds to the estimation of the structural function
h_{\circ}
, which constitutes the primary objective in the 2SLS setting.

5.1
Convergence of the inner loop

First, we study the convergence of the outputs of the inner loop algorithm in
Algorithm

1
, with respect to the number of iterations
T
and the number of particles
N_{z}
, towards the optima
\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}),\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})
for a fixed
\mu_{x}
.
As emphasized in the previous section, the inner-loop optimization corresponds to standard mean-field Langevin dynamics. Consequently, we can directly leverage the existing results on MFLD reviewed in
Section

3
. To this end, we first establish in the following lemma that the global optima
\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}),\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})
satisfy a log-Sobolev inequality.

Lemma 5.1

(Log-Sobolev inequality of the inner-loop)
.

Suppose
2
holds.
For any fixed
\mu_{x}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}})
,
both
\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})
and
{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})
satisfy a Log-Sobolev inequality with constant
C_{\mathrm{LSI},z}=\frac{\zeta_{1}}{2\sigma_{1}}\exp(-\frac{16R^{2}}{\zeta_{1}\sigma_{1}}\sqrt{2d_{z}/\pi})
.

Proof.

The proof is a direct application of Lemma 5 of
73
which itself is based on the contraction principle
(
3
, Proposition
5.4.3)
.
∎

Remark 5.2
.

The Log-Sobolev constant is independent of the Lagrange multiplier
\lambda
. This shall be contrasted with the Log-Sobolev constant
C_{\mathrm{LSI},x}
of the outer-loop optimization, which gets exponentially small as
\lambda
increases, as detailed in
5.6
.

In addition, the linear convexity of the objectives
\mu_{z}\mapsto F_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})
and
\mu_{z}\mapsto L_{\lambda}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})
for any fixed
\mu_{x}
have already been proved in
4.1
and
4.8
.
Therefore, following Theorem 1 of
57
, combining Eq. (
12
) and Eq. (
13
), we obtain the following convergence results.

Proposition 5.3

(Inner-loop convergence towards
\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})
)
.

Suppose
1
and
2
hold.
Given a fixed
\mu_{x}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}})
.
Let
\mathscr{Z}=\{z^{(i)}\}_{i=1}^{N_{z}}
be the first output of the inner-loop algorithm
InnerLoop
(
\mu_{x}
,
T
,
\alpha
,
\beta
,
\lambda
,
\sigma_{1}
), detailed in
Algorithm

1
, where the initial
N_{z}
particles are sampled i.i.d from some distribution
\mu_{z,0}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}})
.
Denote
\mu_{z}^{(N_{z})}
as the joint distribution of these
N_{z}
particles
\mathscr{Z}
.
Suppose the step size
\alpha
satisfies
\alpha\leq\frac{1}{\zeta_{1}}
.
For any
T>0
,

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma_{1}}{N_{z}}\mathrm{KL}\left(\mu_{z}^{(N_{z})},(\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))^{\otimes N_{z}}\right)\leq\frac{R^{2}}{N_{z}}+\frac{\delta_{\alpha}}{C_{\mathrm{LSI},z}\sigma_{1}}+\exp(-C_{\mathrm{LSI},z}\sigma_{1}\alpha T)\Delta_{0}^{(N_{z})}.

Here,
\Delta_{0}^{(N_{z})}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathscr{Z}\sim\mu_{z,0}^{\otimes N_{z}}}[F_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{\mathscr{Z}})]+\sigma_{1}\mathrm{Ent}(\mu_{z,0})-\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))
represent the approximation error at initialization, and
\delta_{\alpha}=8\alpha(C_{2}^{2}+\zeta_{1}^{2})(\alpha C_{1}^{2}+\sigma_{1}d_{z})+32\alpha^{2}\zeta_{1}^{2}(C_{2}^{2}+\zeta_{1}^{2})(\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\mu_{z,0}}[\|z\|_{2}^{2}]+\frac{1}{\zeta_{1}}(\frac{C_{1}^{2}}{4\zeta_{1}}+\sigma_{1}d_{z}))
represents the time discretization error. The expectation above is taken with respect to the randomness of the initial particles and injected Gaussian noise at each iteration. The constants
C_{1}=2R^{2}
and
C_{2}=R+R^{2}
.

Proposition 5.4

(Inner-loop convergence towards
\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})
)
.

Suppose
1
and
2
hold.
Given a fixed
\mu_{x}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}})
and a fixed
\lambda>0
.
Let
\tilde{\mathscr{Z}}=\{\tilde{z}^{(i)}\}_{i=1}^{N_{z}}
be the second output of the inner-loop algorithm
InnerLoop
(
\mu_{x}
,
T
,
\alpha
,
\beta
,
\lambda
,
\sigma_{1}
), detailed in
Algorithm

1
, where the initial
N_{z}
particles are sampled i.i.d from some distribution
\tilde{\mu}_{z,0}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}})
.
Denote
\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{(N_{z})}
as the joint distribution of these
N_{z}
particles
\tilde{\mathscr{Z}}
.
Suppose the step size
\beta
satisfies
\beta\leq\frac{1}{\lambda\zeta_{1}}
.
For any
T>0
,

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda\sigma_{1}}{N_{z}}\mathrm{KL}\left(\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{(N_{z})},(\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))^{\otimes N_{z}}\right)\leq\frac{\lambda R^{2}}{N_{z}}+\frac{\delta_{\beta}}{C_{\mathrm{LSI},z}\lambda\sigma_{1}}+\exp(-C_{\mathrm{LSI},z}\lambda\sigma_{1}\beta T)\tilde{\Delta}_{0}^{(N_{z})}.

Here,
\tilde{\Delta}_{0}^{(N_{z})}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathscr{Z}\sim\tilde{\mu}_{z,0}^{\otimes N_{z}}}[U_{2}(\mu_{\mathscr{Z}})+\lambda F_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{\mathscr{Z}})]+\lambda\sigma_{2}\mathrm{Ent}(\tilde{\mu}_{z,0})-(U_{2}(\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))+\lambda\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{x},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})))
represent the approximation error at initialization, and
\delta_{\beta}=8\beta\lambda^{3}(C_{2}^{2}+\zeta_{1}^{2})(\beta C_{1}^{2}+\sigma_{1}d_{z})+32\beta^{2}\lambda^{4}\zeta_{1}^{2}(C_{2}^{2}+\zeta_{1}^{2})(\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\mu_{z,0}}[\|z\|_{2}^{2}]+\frac{1}{\zeta_{1}}(\frac{C_{1}^{2}}{4\zeta_{1}}+\sigma_{1}d_{z}))
represents the time discretization error. The expectation above is taken with respect to the randomness of the initial particles and injected Gaussian noise at each iteration. The constants
C_{1}=2R^{2}
and
C_{2}=R+R^{2}
.

The proofs of these two propositions are omitted as they are straight forward applications of Theorem 1 of
57
which has been reviewed in
Section

3
. Note that in
5.4
, the dependence on the Lagrange multiplier
\lambda
arises because the both
\ell_{2}
and entropic regularizations have been rescaled by
\lambda
, see Eq. (
17
).
Since the initial
N_{z}
particles are sampled i.i.d from some distributions
\mu_{z,0}
and
\tilde{\mu}_{z,0}
, the initial approximation error terms
\Delta_{0}^{(N_{z})}
and
\tilde{\Delta}_{0}^{(N_{z})}\lambda^{-1}
with fixed
\lambda>0
are uniformly bounded for any
\mu_{x}
as long as
\mu_{z,0}
and
\tilde{\mu}_{z,0}
have finite second moment and finite entropy.

Remark 5.5

(Iteration and particle complexity of the inner-loop)
.

By
5.3
, in order to achieve
N_{z}^{-1}\mathrm{KL}(\mu_{z}^{(N_{z})},(\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))^{\otimes N_{z}})\leq\delta<1
with
\delta
sufficiently small, it suffices to choose the step size
\alpha\lesssim\delta\sigma_{1}C_{\mathrm{LSI},z}\zeta_{1}^{-2}d_{z}^{-1}
, which yields the following iteration and sample complexity:

\displaystyle T\geq\frac{\log(\delta^{-1})}{\delta}\frac{\zeta_{1}^{2}d_{z}}{(C_{\mathrm{LSI},z}\sigma_{1})^{2}},\quad N_{z}\geq\frac{1}{\delta}\frac{1}{\sigma_{1}}.

(19)

Similarly, to achieve
N_{z}^{-1}\mathrm{KL}(\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{(N_{z})},(\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))^{\otimes N_{z}})\leq\delta<1
, it suffices to choose the step size
\beta\leq\lambda^{-1}\delta\sigma_{1}C_{\mathrm{LSI},z}\zeta_{1}^{-2}d_{z}^{-1}
which results in the same iteration and particle complexity as Eq. (
19
). The equality of complexities for the two inner-loop problems in Eq. (
16
) and Eq. (
17
) follows immediately from the fact that the latter’s objective is roughly a rescaled version of the former’s objective by a factor of
\lambda
, and both share the same Log–Sobolev constant (see
5.1
).
We explicitly show the dependence on the log-Sobolev constant
C_{\mathrm{LSI},z}
to emphasize the dependence through it on the dimension
d_{z}
.

5.2
Convergence of the outer loop

In this section, we analyze the convergence of the outer loop in
Algorithm

2
, namely the proposed
F
2
BMLD
algorithm, towards the global optimum
\mu_{x,\lambda}^{\ast}
of (

λ

-penalty
) for a fixed
\lambda>0
.
Recall the definition of the two functionals
L_{\lambda}
and
\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}
in Eq. (
15
) and (

λ

-penalty
):

\displaystyle L_{\lambda}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})=F_{2}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})+\lambda\left(\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})-\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))\right),\text{ }\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})=L_{\lambda}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})+\sigma_{2}\mathrm{Ent}(\mu_{x}).

In practice, the stage I solution
\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})
is learned in the inner-loop (
Algorithm

1
) via mean field Langevin dynamics. To make explicit this dependence on
\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})
, we extend both functionals
L_{\lambda},\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}
to mappings from the product space
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}})\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}})\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}})
to
\mathbb{R}
:

\displaystyle L_{\lambda}(\mu_{x},\tilde{\mu}_{z},\mu_{z})

\displaystyle:=F_{2}(\mu_{x},\tilde{\mu}_{z})+\lambda\cdot\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{x},\tilde{\mu}_{z})-\lambda\cdot\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})

(20)

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}(\mu_{x},\tilde{\mu}_{z},\mu_{z})

\displaystyle:=L_{\lambda}(\mu_{x},\tilde{\mu}_{z},\mu_{z})+\sigma_{2}\mathrm{Ent}(\mu_{x}).

(21)

The outer-loop
Algorithm

2
is a space- and
time-discretized implementation of mean field Langevin dynamics of the functional
\mu_{x}\mapsto L_{\lambda}(\mu_{x},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))
. As a first step in analyzing its convergence, and following the framework reviewed in
Section

3
, we establish that the global optimum
\mu_{x,\lambda}^{\ast}=\arg\min_{\mu_{x}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}})}\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}(\mu_{x},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))
satisfies a log-Sobolev inequality, as stated in the following lemma.

Lemma 5.6

(Log-Sobolev constant of the outer-loop)
.

Suppose
2
holds.
For any fixed
\lambda>0
,
\mu_{x,\lambda}^{\ast}
satisfies a log-Sobolev inequality with constant
C_{\mathrm{LSI},x}=\frac{\zeta_{2}}{2\sigma_{2}}\exp(-16\frac{\lambda^{2}R^{2}}{\zeta_{2}\sigma_{2}}\sqrt{2d_{x}/\pi})
.

Proof.

The proof is a direct application of Lemma 5 of
73
which itself is based on the contraction principle
(
3
, Proposition
5.4.3)
.
∎

Remark 5.7
.

Comparing the LSI constant
C_{\mathrm{LSI},z}
for the inner loop (proved in
5.1
