) with the LSI constant
C_{\mathrm{LSI},x}
for the outer loop (proved in
5.6
), we observe that
C_{\mathrm{LSI},z}
is independent of the Lagrange multiplier
\lambda
, whereas
C_{\mathrm{LSI},x}
deteriorates exponentially as
\lambda
increases. This discrepancy arises because the vector field of the outer loop is scaled by
\lambda
(see Eq. (
4.2
)) , while the entropic and
\ell_{2}
regularizations do not.

Define
\mu_{z,s}^{(N_{z})}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}((\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}})^{N_{z}})
(resp.
\tilde{\mu}_{z,s}^{(N_{z})}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}((\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}})^{N_{z}})
) as the joint distribution of the
N_{z}
particles
\mathscr{Z}_{s}=[z_{s}^{(1)},\ldots,z_{s}^{(N_{z})}]
(resp.
\tilde{\mathscr{Z}}_{s}=[\tilde{z}_{s}^{(1)},\ldots,\tilde{z}_{s}^{(N_{z})}]
) which are the output of the inner loop algorithm at time
s
. The corresponding empirical distributions are
\tilde{\mu}_{\mathscr{Z},s}=\frac{1}{N_{z}}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{z}}\delta_{\tilde{z}_{s}^{(j)}}
and
\mu_{\mathscr{Z},s}=\frac{1}{N_{z}}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{z}}\delta_{z_{s}^{(j)}}
.
Define
\mu_{x,s}^{(N_{x})}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}((\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}})^{N_{x}})
as the joint distribution of the
N_{x}
particles
\mathscr{X}_{s}=[x_{s}^{(1)},\ldots,x_{s}^{(N_{x})}]
of the outer loop algorithm at time
s
.
The corresponding empirical distribution is
\mu_{\mathscr{X},s}=\frac{1}{N_{x}}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{x}}\delta_{x_{s}^{(j)}}
.
Following the procedures on the non-asymptotic convergence bound of MFLD reviewed in
Section

3
, we introduce the following auxiliary functionals
\mathcal{P}_{2}((\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}})^{N_{x}})\to\mathbb{R}
:

\displaystyle L_{\lambda}^{(N_{x})}(\mu_{x}^{(N_{x})})

\displaystyle:=N_{x}\mathbb{E}_{\mathscr{X}\sim\mu_{x}^{(N_{x})}}[F_{2}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}}))]+\lambda\cdot N_{x}\mathbb{E}_{\mathscr{X}\sim\mu_{x}^{(N_{x})}}[\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}}))]

\displaystyle\qquad-\lambda\cdot N_{x}\mathbb{E}_{\mathscr{X}\sim\mu_{x}^{(N_{x})}}[\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}}))]

(22)

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}^{(N_{x})}(\mu_{x}^{(N_{x})})

\displaystyle:=L_{\lambda}^{(N_{x})}(\mu_{x}^{(N_{x})})+\sigma_{1}\mathrm{Ent}(\mu_{x}^{(N_{x})}).

(23)

Next, we are about to inspect whether the key inequalities in MFLD, namely Eq. (
10
) and Eq. (
11
) hold in the current context. Unfortunately, the mapping
\mu_{x}\mapsto L_{\lambda}(\mu_{x},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))
is
no longer
linear convex due to the nested mapping.

A direct consequence of the lack of convexity is that the Bregman divergence
B_{L_{\lambda}}
associated with the mapping
\mu_{x}\mapsto L_{\lambda}(\mu_{x},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))
is
no longer
positive. For any
\mu_{x},\mu_{x}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}})
,

\displaystyle B_{L_{\lambda}}(\mu_{x},\mu_{x}^{\prime}):=L_{\lambda}(\mu_{x},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))

\displaystyle-L_{\lambda}(\mu_{x}^{\prime},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}^{\prime}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\quad-\smallint\delta_{\mu_{x}}L_{\lambda}(\mu_{x}^{\prime},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}^{\prime}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}^{\prime}))\;\mathrm{d}(\mu_{x}-\mu_{x}^{\prime})

(24)

Here,
\delta_{\mu_{x}}
denotes taking the first variation of the mapping
\mu_{x}\mapsto L_{\lambda}(\mu_{x},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))
.
Fortunately, however, we can prove in the following lemma that the Bregman divergence of
\mu_{x},\mu_{x}^{\prime}
is lower bounded by the negative squared total variation distance of
\mu_{x},\mu_{x}^{\prime}
.

Lemma 5.8

(Lower-bound on the Bregman divergence)
.

Suppose
2
holds.
Then, we have
B_{L_{\lambda}}(\mu_{x},\mu_{x}^{\prime})\geq-\frac{R\lambda}{4\sigma_{1}}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\left(\smallint\Psi_{\mathbf{a}}\;\mathrm{d}(\mu_{x}-\mu_{x}^{\prime})\right)^{2}\right]\geq-\frac{R^{3}\lambda}{4\sigma_{1}}\mathrm{TV}^{2}(\mu_{x},\mu_{x}^{\prime})
.

The proof can be found in
Section

9.2.2
.
The above lemma implies that although the functional
\mu_{x}\mapsto L_{\lambda}(\mu_{x},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))
is not convex, it is actually
weakly convex
with respect to the total variation norm.
This is directly analogous to the Euclidean setting, where the Bregman divergence of a differentiable function measures the deviation from its linear approximation, and the existence of a quadratic lower bound is equivalent to weak convexity
(
10
)
. The weak convexity parameter deteriorates as
\lambda
increases, but improves as
\sigma_{1}
increases.

We are now ready to establish the two key inequalities of MFLD in our setting, namely Eq. (
10
) and Eq. (
11
).

Proposition 5.9

(Defective Bregman divergence gap)
.

Suppose
2
holds and let
\mathfrak{c}>0
.
Assume that
\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\mathfrak{c}\geq 4R^{3}\lambda
.
Then, for any
\mu_{x}^{(N_{x})}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}((\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}})^{N_{x}})
, we have

\displaystyle N_{x}^{-1}\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}^{(N_{x})}(\mu_{x}^{(N_{x})})-\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}(\mu_{x,\lambda}^{\ast},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{*}(\mu_{x,\lambda}^{\ast}),\mu_{z}^{*}(\mu_{x,\lambda}^{\ast}))\geq N_{x}^{-1}\frac{\sigma_{2}}{2}\mathrm{KL}(\mu_{x}^{(N_{x})},(\mu_{x,\lambda}^{\ast})^{\otimes N_{x}})-\frac{2R^{3}\lambda\mathfrak{c}}{\sigma_{1}}-\frac{2R^{3}\lambda}{N_{x}\sigma_{1}}.

The proof can be found in
Section

9.2.3
, which is based on
5.8
and Proposition 1 in
57
.

Proposition 5.10

(Defective uniform log-Sobolev inequality)
.

Suppose
2
holds and let
\mathfrak{c}>0
.
Let
\mu_{\ast}^{(N_{x})}=\arg\min_{\mu^{(N_{x})}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}((\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}})^{N_{x}})}\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}^{(N_{x})}(\mu^{(N_{x})})
.
Assume that
\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\mathfrak{c}\geq 4R^{3}\lambda
.
Then, for any
\mu_{x}^{(N_{x})}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}((\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}})^{N_{x}})
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}^{(N_{x})}(\mu_{x}^{(N_{x})})}{N_{x}}-\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}(\mu_{x,\lambda}^{\ast},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{*}(\mu_{x,\lambda}^{\ast}),\mu_{z}^{*}(\mu_{x,\lambda}^{\ast}))\leq\frac{\sigma_{2}\mathrm{FI}\left(\mu_{x}^{(N_{x})},\mu_{\ast}^{(N_{x})}\right)}{C_{\mathrm{LSI},x}N_{x}}+\frac{2\lambda R^{2}(\frac{R}{\sigma_{1}}+1)}{N_{x}}+\frac{\mathfrak{c}^{2}C_{\mathrm{LSI},x}}{8}.

The proof can be found in
Section

9.2.4
.

5.9
and
5.10
serve as counterparts of the key inequalities Eq. (
10
) and Eq. (
11
), which underpin the convergence analysis of mean-field Langevin dynamics (see
Section

3
).

Remark 5.11
.

The main difficulty in our setting, as repeatedly emphasized, is the non-convexity of the functional
\mu_{x}\mapsto L_{\lambda}(\mu_{x},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))
. Nevertheless,
5.8
shows that this functional is weakly convex with respect to the total variation norm. This allows us to overcome the lack of convexity by imposing the condition
\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\geq 2R^{3}\lambda\mathfrak{c}
, where a larger entropic regularizations
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}
improves convexity of the problem.
Here
\mathfrak{c}>0
is a free slack parameter introduced in the analysis: smaller values yield sharper bounds but require stronger entropic regularization, while larger values loosen the bound but relax the condition (see
Theorem

5.12
). Overall, our results demonstrate that the analysis of MFLD extends to more general weakly convex functionals, provided an additional condition is imposed on the scales of the
\ell_{2}
and entropic regularization.

With the two propositions in place, we are now prepared to establish the convergence of the proposed algorithm
F
2
BFLD
in the theorem below.

Theorem 5.12

(Convergence bound)
.

Suppose
1
and
2
hold. Let
\mathfrak{c}>0
and assume that
\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\mathfrak{c}\geq 4R^{3}\lambda
.
Suppose the step size
\gamma\leq\zeta_{2}^{-1}
.
Denote
\mathcal{H}(s):=N_{x}^{-1}\mathbb{E}[\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}^{(N_{x})}(\mu_{x,s}^{(N_{x})})]-\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}(\mu_{x,\lambda}^{\ast},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{*}(\mu_{x,\lambda}^{\ast}),\mu_{z}^{*}(\mu_{x,\lambda}^{\ast}))
for any
s\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
where the expectation is taken over the randomness of the initial i.i.d
N_{x}
particles samples from
\mu_{x,0}
, the initial
N_{z}
particles in each inner-loop, and the injected Gaussian noise at each iteration.

For any number of iterations
S\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
, we have

\displaystyle\quad\mathcal{H}(S)\lesssim\exp\left(-\frac{\sigma_{2}C_{\mathrm{LSI},x}S\gamma}{4}\right)\mathcal{H}(0)+\frac{\lambda R^{2}(\frac{R}{\sigma_{1}}+1)}{N_{x}}+\frac{\lambda^{2}R^{4}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\mathfrak{KL}}{N_{z}}}+\sqrt{\frac{\tilde{\mathfrak{KL}_{\lambda}}}{N_{z}}}+\frac{1}{N_{z}}\right)}{\sigma_{2}C_{\mathrm{LSI},x}}

\displaystyle+\frac{\lambda^{2}R^{4}+\zeta_{2}^{2}+\frac{\lambda^{2}R^{6}}{\sigma_{1}}}{\sigma_{2}C_{\mathrm{LSI},x}}\left(\gamma^{2}(\zeta_{2}^{2}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{x,0}}[\|x\|^{2}]+\lambda^{2}R^{2})+\gamma\sigma_{2}d_{x}\right)+\mathfrak{c}^{2}C_{\mathrm{LSI},x}.

(25)

\mathfrak{KL}
and
\tilde{\mathfrak{KL}}_{\lambda}
in the last term of the first line above denote the KL upper bound of the convergence results in the inner-loop presented in
5.3
and
5.4
, respectively.

The proof can be found in
Section

9.2.1
.
The convergence bound in Eq. (
25
) consists of five terms. The first term,
\exp(-\sigma_{2}C_{\mathrm{LSI},x}S\gamma)\mathcal{H}(0)
, decays exponentially fast with the number of iterations
S
, as a consequence of the uniform log-Sobolev inequality.
Since the initial
N_{x}
particles are sampled i.i.d from some distribution
\mu_{x,0}
, the initial approximation error term
\mathcal{H}(0)
with a fixed
\lambda
is finite as long as
\mu_{x,0}
has finite second moment and finite entropy.
The second term,
\mathcal{O}(N_{x}^{-1})
, accounts for the particle approximation error, while the fourth term,
\mathcal{O}(\gamma^{2}+\gamma\sigma_{2})
, corresponds to the time discretization error. The third term, involving
\mathfrak{KL}
and
\tilde{\mathfrak{KL}}_{\lambda}
, reflects the error from the inner loop, which is a unique term in our double-loop algorithm
F
2
BMLD
. The inner-loop error arises here because the inner loop optima
\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}),\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})
, that show up in computing the Wasserstein gradient of the outer loop, are approximated by the outputs of the inner-loop algorithm.
Finally, the fifth term
\mathfrak{c}^{2}C_{\mathrm{LSI},x}
is an artifact of the proof that arises due to the weak convexity.
The parameter
\mathfrak{c}>0
acts as a slack variable: choosing a smaller
\mathfrak{c}
yields sharper convergence bounds but requires stronger entropic regularization
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
to satisfy the condition
\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\mathfrak{c}\geq 4R^{3}\lambda
, while larger
\mathfrak{c}
relaxes this condition at the expense of a looser bound.

Remark 5.13

(Uniform convergence of the neural network)
.

Our convergence bound in
Theorem

5.12
on
\mathcal{H}(S)
can be translated into an convergence bound on the neural network output via
5.9
and Proposition 1 of
57
.
Define
\hat{h}_{S}(\mathbf{a})=\frac{1}{N_{x}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{x}}\Psi_{\mathbf{a}}(x_{S}^{(i)})
where
\{x_{S}^{(i)}\}_{i=1}^{N_{x}}
are
N_{x}
particles which are the output of
F
2
BMLD
. Define
h_{\ast,\lambda}(\mathbf{a})=\smallint\Psi_{\mathbf{a}}(x)\;\mathrm{d}\mu_{x,\lambda}^{\ast}(x)
where
\mu_{x,\lambda}^{\ast}
is the global optimum of (

λ

-penalty
) for a fixed
\lambda>0
. Then, for any
\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{X}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\hat{h}_{S}(\mathbf{a})-h_{\ast,\lambda}(\mathbf{a})\right)^{2}\right]\leq 8R^{2}\sqrt{\sigma_{2}^{-1}\mathcal{H}(S)+\frac{R^{3}\lambda\mathfrak{c}}{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}+N_{x}^{-1}\frac{R^{3}\lambda}{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}}+\frac{4R^{2}}{N_{x}}.

(26)

Here, the expectation is taken over the randomness in the proposed algorithm
F
2
BMLD
.

Remark 5.14

(Iteration and particle complexity of
F
2
BMLD
)
.

For simplicity, we consider
\lambda\geq 1
and
C_{\mathrm{LSI},x}\leq 1
which are often met in practice. To reach
\mathbb{E}[(\hat{h}_{S}(\mathbf{a})-h_{\ast,\lambda}(\mathbf{a}))^{2}]\leq\delta<1
with
\delta
sufficiently small, it suffices to reach
\mathcal{H}(S)\leq\delta^{2}
and hence suffices to take the slack parameter
\mathfrak{c}=\delta
. Then, it suffices to
take the step size
\gamma\lesssim\delta^{2}(\lambda^{2}+\zeta_{2}^{2}+\sigma_{1}^{-1}\lambda^{2})^{-1}\sigma_{2}C_{\mathrm{LSI},x}d_{x}^{-1}
, which yields the following iteration and sample complexity:

\displaystyle S\geq\frac{\log(\delta^{-1})}{\delta^{2}}\frac{(\lambda^{2}+\zeta_{2}^{2}+\sigma_{1}^{-1}\lambda^{2})d_{x}}{C_{\mathrm{LSI},x}^{2}\sigma_{2}^{2}},\quad N_{x}\geq\frac{1}{\delta^{2}}\frac{\lambda(\sigma_{1}^{-1}+1)}{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}},

and the following iteration and particle complexity of the inner loop as per
5.5
:

\displaystyle T\geq\frac{\log(\delta^{-1})}{\delta^{4}}\frac{\lambda^{4}}{\sigma_{2}^{4}C_{\mathrm{LSI},x}^{2}}\frac{\zeta_{1}^{2}d_{z}}{\sigma_{1}^{2}C_{\mathrm{LSI},z}^{2}},\quad N_{z}\geq\frac{1}{\delta^{4}}\frac{\lambda^{4}}{\sigma_{2}^{4}C_{\mathrm{LSI},x}^{2}}\frac{1}{\sigma_{1}}.

Note that the iteration complexities in both stages improve as the entropic regularizations
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}
increase, and deteriorate as the log Sobolev constants
C_{\mathrm{LSI},x},C_{\mathrm{LSI},z}
decrease. The sample complexity of
N_{x}
is independent of log Sobolev constants
C_{\mathrm{LSI},x},C_{\mathrm{LSI},z}
as a result of using the state-of-the-art propagation of chaos bound of MFLD from
57
.

6
Generalization of
F
2
BMLD

In this section, we study the statistical properties of the optimal solution obtained via Lagrangian formulation (

λ

-penalty
) when the objectives are computed with finite i.i.d samples from the joint data generating distribution
P
over
(\mathbf{a},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{w})
.
Our analysis focuses on the
generalization
error of the mean field network
h_{\lambda}^{\ast}:\mathbf{a}\mapsto\int\Psi(\mathbf{a},x)\mathrm{d}\mu_{x,\lambda}^{\ast}
induced by the optimal solution
\mu_{x,\lambda}^{\ast}
to (

λ

-penalty
).
Together with the optimization error studied in
Section

5
, our analysis provides a
complete
characterization of the performance of the proposed algorithm
F
2
BMLD
.

Denote the corresponding marginal distributions as
P_{A},P_{W},P_{WA},P_{WY}
and denote the conditional distribution as
P_{X\mid Z}
.
In particular, given
m
i.i.d samples
\{\mathbf{w}_{i},\mathbf{a}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}\sim P_{WA}
in stage I and
n
i.i.d samples
\{\mathbf{w}_{i},\mathbf{y}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}\sim P_{WY}
in stage II, the objectives
\mathscr{F}_{1},\mathscr{F}_{2}
in both stages now become

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{1}{2m}\left(\smallint\Psi(\mathbf{w}_{i},z)\mathrm{d}\mu_{z}-\smallint\Psi(\mathbf{a}_{i},x)\mathrm{d}\mu_{x}\right)^{2}+\frac{\zeta_{1}}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{z}}[\|z\|^{2}]+\sigma_{1}\mathrm{Ent}(\mu_{z}),

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}_{2}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{2n}\left(\smallint\Psi(\mathbf{w}_{i},z)\mathrm{d}\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})-\mathbf{y}_{i}\right)^{2}+\frac{\zeta_{2}}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{x}}[\|x\|^{2}]+\sigma_{2}\mathrm{Ent}(\mu_{x}).

(27)

Recall from Eq. (
1
) that the conditional expectation operator

T:L^{2}(P_{A})\to L^{2}(P_{W})
defined as
T:f\mapsto\mathbb{E}[f(A)\mid W]
. In the remainder of this section, we use the full notation
\Psi(\mathbf{a},x)
and
\Psi(\mathbf{w},z)
to emphasize the dependence on the network inputs
\mathbf{a},\mathbf{w}
.

To start with, we make a few assumptions on the regression targets in both stages.

Assumption 3

(Stage II well-specifiedness)
.

The structural function
h_{\circ}
belongs to a KL restricted Barron space
\mathcal{B}_{M_{x}}:=\{\smallint\Psi(\cdot,x)\mathrm{d}\mu_{x}(x)\mid\mathrm{KL}(\mu_{x},\nu_{x})\leq M_{x}\}
, where
\nu_{x}=\mathcal{N}(0,\zeta_{2}\sigma_{2}^{-1}\mathrm{Id}_{d_{x}})
. That is, there exists a measure
\mu_{x}^{\circ}\in\mathcal{B}_{M_{x}}
such that
h_{\circ}(\cdot)=\smallint\Psi(\cdot,x)\mathrm{d}\mu_{x}^{\circ}
.

Assumption 4

(Stage I well-specifiedness)
.

For any
\mu_{x}
with
\mathrm{KL}(\mu_{x},\nu_{x})\leq\mathrm{KL}(\mu_{x}^{\circ},\nu_{x})+2\sigma_{2}^{-1}R^{2}
, the conditional expectation
T[\smallint\Psi(\cdot,x)\mathrm{d}\mu_{x}(x)](\mathbf{w})=\smallint\mathbb{E}[\Psi(A,x)\mid Z=\mathbf{w}]\;\mathrm{d}\mu_{x}(x)
belongs to a KL restricted Barron space
\mathcal{B}_{M_{z}}:=\{\smallint\Psi(\cdot,z)\mathrm{d}\mu_{z}(z)\mid\mathrm{KL}(\mu_{z},\nu_{z})\leq M_{z}\}
, where
\nu_{z}=\mathcal{N}(0,\zeta_{1}\sigma_{1}^{-1}\mathrm{Id}_{d_{z}})
.
That is, there exists a measure
\mu_{z}^{\circ}(\mu_{x})\in\mathcal{B}_{M_{z}}
such that
T[\smallint\Psi(\cdot,x)\mathrm{d}\mu_{x}(x)](\mathbf{w})=\smallint\Psi(\cdot,z)\mathrm{d}\mu_{z}^{\circ}(\mu_{x})
.

3
is standard in studying the generalization error of
two-layer mean field neural networks

(
19
;
75
)
.
The KL divergence upper bound
M_{x}
quantifies the difficulty for a target function to be learned by a mean field neural network.

4
states that for any suitably regular distribution
\mu_{x}
, its associated mean-field neural network, once smoothed by the compact operator
T
, can again be expressed as a mean-field neural network.
To give a concrete example, suppose
d_{x}=d_{z}
hence
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}})=\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}})
: if the conditional density of
P_{X\mid Z}
is translation-invariant, i.e.,
p(\mathbf{a}\mid\mathbf{w})=p(\mathbf{w}-\mathbf{a})
, then the condition in
4
is satisfied with
\Psi(\mathbf{w},z)=\int\Psi(\mathbf{a},z)p(\mathbf{w}-\mathbf{a})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{a}
,
\mu_{z}^{\circ}(\mu_{x})=\mu_{x}
and
M_{z}=M_{x}+2\sigma_{2}^{-1}R^{2}
.
The KL constraint on
\mu_{x}
in
4
arises from
9.7
, which proves that
\mathrm{KL}(\mu_{x,\lambda}^{\ast},\nu_{x})\leq\mathrm{KL}(\mu_{x}^{\circ},\nu_{x})+2\sigma_{2}^{-1}R^{2}
for any
\lambda>0
.
This KL constraint is necessary as it rules out irregular
\mu_{x}
, such as dirac delta distributions.

Theorem 6.1

(Generalization bound)
.

Suppose
1
,
2
,
3
and
4
hold.
For
\lambda>0
, let
\mu_{x,\lambda}^{\ast}
be the optimal solution to the Lagrangian problem (

λ

-penalty
) and
h_{\ast,\lambda}(\mathbf{a})=\smallint\Psi(\mathbf{a},x)\;\mathrm{d}\mu_{x,\lambda}^{\ast}(x)
be its associated mean field neural network.
Then, with
P^{\otimes(m+n)}
probability at least
1-8\delta
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{P_{W}}\left[\Big((Th_{\ast,\lambda})(W)-(Th_{\circ})(W)\Big)^{2}\right]\lesssim\sigma_{2}M_{x}+\sigma_{1}M_{z}+\frac{R^{2}(R+M)^{2}}{\sigma_{1}\lambda}+R^{2}\sqrt{\frac{\log(\delta^{-1})}{m}}

\displaystyle\quad+R^{2}\sqrt{\frac{M_{z}+\frac{R^{2}}{\sigma_{1}}}{m}}+(R+M)^{2}\sqrt{\frac{\log(\delta^{-1})}{n}}+R(R+M)\sqrt{\frac{M_{x}+\frac{R^{2}}{\sigma_{2}}}{n}}.

The proof can be found in
Section

9.3
. The proof is a non-trivial adaptation of existing generalization bounds of mean field neural networks into our setting of bilevel optimization and its Lagrangian formulation, which requires careful control of the interaction between two stages. The
final generalization bound can be dissected into four components:
\sigma_{2}M_{x}+\sigma_{1}M_{z}
captures the increased complexity of the KL-restricted Barron spaces in
3
and
4
;
\mathcal{O}(\frac{1}{\sigma_{1}\lambda})
reflects the approximation error introduced by the Lagrangian formulation shown in
Theorem

4.5
; and
1/\sqrt{n},1/\sqrt{m}
correspond to the sample complexities of stage I and stage II, respectively.

Some of the existing generalization results for 2SLS, either with fixed features
(
14
;
53
)
or with adaptive features
(
37
)
, are expressed in terms of the unprojected norm
\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}(P_{A})}
. In contrast, our bound is stated with respect to the projected norm
\|T(\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(P_{W})}
which is a weaker metric because
T
is bounded.
Such a bound in weaker metric is to be expected, since we do not impose these strong structural assumptions on
T
—such as measures of ill-posedness
(
14
)
or link conditions
(
17
)
—which are generally difficult to verify in practice.

Remark 6.2

(Trade-off on
\lambda
between optimization and generalization)
.

A closer inspection of the role of the Lagrange multiplier
\lambda
in the optimization bound of
Theorem

5.12
and the generalization bound of
Theorem

6.1
reveals a clear
trade-off
. For the optimization bound, smaller values of
\lambda
are preferable, as they yield a weaker convexity parameter (
5.8
) and smaller Lipschitz constants, thereby reducing both the time-discretization error and the contribution from the inner-loop error. In contrast, for the generalization bound, larger values of
\lambda
are favorable, since they make the Lagrangian relaxation more faithful to the original bilevel optimization problem (
Theorem

4.5
). Taken together, the optimization bound of
Theorem

5.12
and the generalization bound of
Theorem

6.1
lead to a combined error bound. Due to the inherent trade-off between these two effects, we do not expect any choice of
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\lambda)
to eliminate the total error.

7
Experiments

In this section, we empirically evaluate our proposed method,
F
2
BMLD
, on the offline policy evaluation (OPE) problem, a fundamental challenge in reinforcement learning
(
72
;
45
)
.
As early as in
11
, it was observed that two-stage least squares (2SLS)—originally developed for instrumental variable regression—can also be applied to estimate the value function in offline reinforcement learning. More recently, OPE has become a standard benchmark for evaluating 2SLS algorithms, either with fixed or adaptive features, as it presents a greater challenge than synthetic causal inference datasets
(
86
;
18
)
.

Formally, consider a reinforcement learning environment
\langle\mathbb{S},\mathbb{B},P,R,\nu_{0},\eta\rangle
, where
\mathbb{S}
is the state space,
\mathbb{B}
is the action space,
P:\mathbb{S}\times\mathbb{B}\times\mathbb{S}\to[0,1]
is the transition kernel,
R:\mathbb{S}\times\mathbb{B}\times\mathbb{S}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is the reward distribution,
\nu_{0}:\mathbb{S}\to[0,1]
is the initial state distribution, and
\eta\in(0,1]
is the discount factor. A policy
\pi
is defined such that
\pi(b\mid s)
is the probability of selecting action
b
in state
s\in\mathbb{S}
. Given a policy
\pi
, the
Q
-function
is defined as

\displaystyle Q^{\pi}(s,b)=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\eta^{t}r_{t}\mid s_{0}=s,b_{0}=b\right]

with
b_{t}\sim\pi\left(\cdot\mid s_{t}\right),s_{t+1}\sim P\left(\cdot\mid s_{t},b_{t}\right),r_{t}\sim R\left(\cdot\mid s_{t},b_{t},s_{t+1}\right)
.
The goal of offline policy evaluation to estimate the expected
Q
-value of a given target policy
\pi
under the initial state distribution, also known as the policy value:

\displaystyle V(\pi)=\mathbb{E}_{s\sim\nu_{0},b\mid s\sim\pi}\left[Q^{\pi}(s,b)\right].

The challenge of OPE, as suggested by its name, is that direct interaction with the environment is not permitted. Instead, one must rely on an existing pre-collected dataset of trajectories tuples
\left(s,b,r,s^{\prime}\right)
to estimate the policy value, and potentially to deduce an optimal policy. Such
offline
datasets are typically generated by one or more unknown behavior policies
\pi_{b}
. One popular family of OPE approaches is to estimate the value function based on the Bellman equation
(
72
)
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[r\mid s,b]=Q(s,b)-\eta\mathbb{E}[Q(s^{\prime},b^{\prime})\mid s,b],

(28)

where the first expectation is taken with respect to the reward distribution, while the second expectation is taken with respect to the policy
\pi
and the transition kernel
P
. Notably, Eq.(
28
) has the same structure as the conditional moment equations in NPIV (Eq.(
1
)) and hence can be solved via 2SLS:
the conditional expectation operator
T
, induced by
\pi(b^{\prime}\mid s^{\prime})\times P(s^{\prime}\mid s,b)
would be learned by samples generated by the behavior policy
\pi_{b}
.

Figure 1
:
Left:
Comparison of DFIV and F2BMLD in terms of target policy value.
Right:
Comparison of DFIV and F2BMLD training trajectories.

We evaluate our proposed
F
2
BMLD
on Cartpole where an agent can move a cart left/right on a plane to keep a balanced
pole upright
(
61
)
.
The original system dynamics are deterministic. To create a stochastic environment, we randomly replace the agent action by a uniformly sampled action with probability
p\in\{0,0.1,0.2,0.3,0.4\}
. The noise level
p
controls the level of confounding effect. The target policy is trained with DQN
(
54
)
, and an offline dataset for OPE is generated by executing the policy in the same environment with an additional random action probability of
0.1
(applied on top of the environment’s randomization
p
). We primarily compare
F
2
BMLD
against DFIV, which represents the state of the art in 2SLS with adaptive neural network features.

For
F
2
BMLD
, we use a learning rate of
10^{-4}
for both inner and outer loops, set the Lagrange multiplier to
\lambda=0.3
(following
67
), use a batch size of
32
, and apply
\ell_{2}
regularization
\zeta_{1}=\zeta_{2}=10^{-5}
together with noise regularization
\sigma_{1}=\sigma_{2}=10^{-2}
. The inner loop is trained for
10
steps per outer iteration, and the outer loop is trained for
50{,}000
iterations, at which point convergence is observed.
For DFIV, we adopt the same hyperparameter settings as
18
3
3

3

The original code provided in
18
relies on old versions of tensorflow and acme which are not compatible with the latest versions. We implemented DFIV from scratch with OpenAI Gymnasium in our repository.
.
To retain consistency, we use a two-layer neural network with hidden layer of width
50
for both methods.
The code to reproduce all the results can be found in
https://github.com/hudsonchen/F2BMLD
.

The empirical results are summarized in
Figure

1
. From
Figure

1
, we observe that
F
2
BMLD
achieves comparable, and in some cases smaller, absolute error than DFIV when estimating the target policy value.
Figure

1
further shows that
F
2
BMLD
exhibits a more stable training trajectory than DFIV. The instability of DFIV is likely caused by propagating the gradient through the ridge regression solution for the final layer.
An additional advantage of
F
2
BMLD
is that it allows a smaller batch size during training than DFIV
(
85
)
.
In our experiments,
F
2
BMLD
used a batch size of
32
, whereas DFIV required a batch size of
1024

(
85
)
. Although our experiments are conducted with two-layer networks, the
F
2
BMLD
algorithm can, in principle, be extended to deeper neural networks, albeit without theoretical guarantees from mean-field Langevin dynamics.
Given the theoretical focus of this paper, we leave a more extensive empirical evaluation on larger and more challenging reinforcement learning benchmarks to future work.

8
Conclusion

We introduced
F
2
BMLD
, a fully first-order bilevel mean-field Langevin dynamics algorithm derived from a Lagrangian reformulation of bilevel optimization. By adopting a lifted perspective over the space of probability measures, we established global convergence guarantees under an extra condition on the noise regularizations
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}
to overcome the non-convexity of the outer level objective. We also provided a generalization bound of the global optimum under access to i.i.d samples, which reveals a trade-off on the Lagrange multiplier
\lambda
.

Following our work, several interesting open problems remain.
(i) Our analysis can, in principle, be extended to study the convergence of standard mean-field Langevin dynamics with weakly convex functionals, thereby allowing a broader class of loss functions.
(ii) A more comprehensive empirical evaluation of
F
2
BMLD
on challenging benchmarks is warranted. While our theory focuses on two-layer neural networks, the algorithm itself readily extends to deeper architectures.
(iii) The lifted perspective provided by mean-field Langevin dynamics could also be applied to study the optimization and generalization of neural networks in NPIV beyond 2SLS, including min–max estimation
(
26
)
.

9
Proofs

9.1
Proofs in
Section

4

9.1.1
Proof of
4.3

Proof of
4.3
.

Given the partial convexity of
\mu_{z}\mapsto U_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})
, the properties of the stage I solution
\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})
have been proved in Proposition 2.5 of
35
, for any
\mu_{x}
. We focus on the properties of the stage II solution
\mu_{x}^{\ast}
. Note that the mapping
\mu_{x}\mapsto U_{2}(\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))
is continuous under the weak convergence topology. To see why, notice that

\displaystyle|U_{2}(\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}^{\prime}))-U_{2}(\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))|

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{\leq}}(R+M)\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\left|\smallint\Psi_{\mathbf{w}}\;\mathrm{d}\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}^{\prime})-\smallint\Psi_{\mathbf{w}}\;\mathrm{d}\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})\right|\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(ii)}}{{\leq}}R(R+M)\mathrm{TV}(\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}^{\prime}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(iii)}}{{\leq}}R(R+M)\sqrt{\mathrm{KL}(\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}^{\prime}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(iv)}}{{\leq}}R(R+M)\sqrt{(4\sigma_{1})^{-1}}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\left(\smallint\Psi_{\mathbf{a}}\;\mathrm{d}(\mu_{x}-\mu_{x}^{\prime})\right)^{2}\right]}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(v)}}{{\leq}}R^{2}(R+M)\sqrt{(4\sigma_{1})^{-1}}W_{2}(\mu_{x},\mu_{x}^{\prime}).

(29)

In the above derivations,
(i)
holds by
10.5
;
(ii)
holds by
2
that
\Psi_{\mathbf{w}}
is bounded by
R
;
(iii)
holds by Pinsker’s inequality;
(iv)
holds by
10.4
and
(v)
holds by
2
that the gradient
\Psi_{\mathbf{w}}
is bounded by
R
.

Since the negative entropy is lower-semicontinuous under the weak convergence topology
(
28
, Lemma 1.4.3)
, the mapping
\mu_{x}\mapsto\mathscr{F}_{2}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))=U_{2}(\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))+\frac{\zeta}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{x}}[\|x\|^{2}]+\sigma_{1}\mathrm{Ent}(\mu_{x})
is also lower-semicontinuous.
Clearly there exists
\bar{\mu}_{x}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}})
such that
\mathscr{F}_{2}(\bar{\mu}_{x},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\bar{\mu}_{x}))=\mathfrak{M}<\infty
. Consider the following subset of
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}})

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}:=\left\{\mu_{x}:\sigma_{1}\mathrm{Ent}(\mu_{x})\leq\mathfrak{M}-\inf_{\mu_{x}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}})}F_{2}(\mu_{x}^{\prime},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}^{\prime}))\right\}.

As a sublevel set of the negative entropy,
\mathcal{S}
is weakly compact, see e.g.
28
. Together with the lower semi-continuity of
\mu_{x}\mapsto\mathscr{F}_{2}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))
, the minimum on
\mathcal{S}
is attained. Notice that for all
\mu_{x}\notin\mathcal{S}
, we have
\mathscr{F}_{2}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))\geq\mathscr{F}_{2}(\bar{\mu}_{x},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\bar{\mu}_{x}))
, so the minimum on
\mathcal{S}
coincides with the global minimum. However, unlike the stage I solution, the stage II solution
\mu_{x}^{\ast}
may be non-unique due to the lack of convexity of the nested mapping
\mu_{x}\mapsto U_{2}(\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))
.
Finally, to ensure
\mathrm{Ent}(\mu_{x})<\infty
and
\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{x}}[\|x\|^{2}]<\infty
, the solution
\mu_{x}^{\ast}
is absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure, and belongs to
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}})
.
∎

9.1.2
Proof of
4.4

Proof of
4.4
.

From Proposition 2.3 of
35
, for any
\mu_{x}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}})
, we know that
\lim_{\sigma_{1}\to 0}W_{2}(\mu_{z,\sigma_{1}}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}),\mu_{z,0}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))=0
. From
10.5
, we know that

|U_{2}(\mu_{z,\sigma_{1}}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))-U_{2}(\mu_{z,0}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))|\leq R(R+M)W_{2}(\mu_{z,\sigma_{1}}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}),\mu_{z,0}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))
.
So we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\sigma_{1}\to 0}\left|U_{2}(\mu_{z,\sigma_{1}}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))-U_{2}(\mu_{z,0}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))\right|=0.

(30)

In the meanwhile, from the continuity of
\mu_{x}\mapsto U_{2}(\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))
in terms of the weak convergence topology proved in Eq. (
29
), for a fixed
\sigma_{1}
and a sequence
(\mu_{x,n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{+}}
converging weakly to
\mu_{x}
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to+\infty}U_{2}(\mu_{z,\sigma_{1}}^{\ast}(\mu_{x,n}))=U_{2}(\mu_{z,\sigma_{1}}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})).

(31)

Now we combine Eq. (
30
) and Eq. (
31
): for a positive sequence
(\sigma_{1,n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{+}}
decreasing to
0
and any sequence of distributions
(\mu_{x,n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{+}}
converging weakly to
\mu_{x}
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to+\infty}U_{2}(\mu_{z,\sigma_{1,n}}^{\ast}(\mu_{x,n}))=U_{2}(\mu_{z,0}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})).

Therefore, for two positive sequences that converge to
0
:
(\sigma_{1,n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{+}}\to 0
and
(\sigma_{2,n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{+}}\to 0
, we have

\lim\inf_{n\to\infty}\mathscr{F}_{2,(\sigma_{1,n},\sigma_{2,n})}(\mu_{x,n})\geq\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathscr{F}_{2,(\sigma_{1,n},0)}(\mu_{x,n})=\mathscr{F}_{2,(0,0)}(\mu_{x})
. On the other hand, we construct a sequence of distributions
(\mu_{x}\ast r_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{+}}
where
r_{n}(x)=\sigma_{2,n}^{-d_{x}/2}r(x/\sqrt{\sigma_{2,n}})
and
r
is the heat kernel. Following the same derivations as in Proposition 2.3 of
35
, we have
\lim\sup_{n\rightarrow+\infty}\mathscr{F}_{2,(\sigma_{1,n},\sigma_{2,n})}(\mu_{x}*r_{n})\leq\mathscr{F}_{2,(0,0)}(\mu_{x})
. So we have concluded the proof of
\Gamma
-convergence.
∎

9.1.3
Proof of
Theorem

4.5

Proof of
Theorem

4.5
.

Recall that
\mathscr{F}_{2}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})=U_{2}(\mu_{z})+\frac{\zeta}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{x}}[\|x\|^{2}]+\sigma_{2}\mathrm{Ent}(\mu_{x})
. From
10.5
, we have, for any fixed
\mu_{x}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}})
,

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}_{2}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})-\mathscr{F}_{2}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))

\displaystyle\geq-(R+M)\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\left|\smallint\Psi_{\mathbf{w}}\;\mathrm{d}\mu_{z}-\smallint\Psi_{\mathbf{w}}\;\mathrm{d}\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})\right|\right]

\displaystyle\geq-(R+M)R\cdot\mathrm{TV}(\mu_{z},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})).

The last inequality holds because
\Psi_{\mathbf{w}}
is bounded from
2
.
From the convexity of
U_{2}
proved in
4.1
and the entropy sandwich theorem proved in Lemma 3.4 of
23
, we have, for any fixed
\mu_{x}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}})
and any
\mu_{z}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}})
,

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})-\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))\geq\sigma_{1}\mathrm{KL}(\mu_{z},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})).

Combine the above two inequalities, for any
\mu_{x},\mu_{z}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\quad\mathscr{F}_{2}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})-\mathscr{F}_{2}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))+\lambda\left(\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})-\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))\right)

(32)

\displaystyle\geq-R(R+M)\mathrm{TV}(\mu_{z},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))+\lambda\sigma_{1}\mathrm{KL}(\mu_{z},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))

\displaystyle\geq-R(R+M)\mathrm{TV}(\mu_{z},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))+2\lambda\sigma_{1}\mathrm{TV}^{2}(\mu_{z},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))

\displaystyle\geq\min_{t\geq 0}-R(R+M)t+2\lambda\sigma_{1}t^{2}=-\frac{R^{2}(R+M)^{2}}{8\lambda\sigma_{1}}.

The second last inequality holds from the Pinsker’s inequality. Therefore, for any
\mu_{x},\mu_{z}
, we have

\displaystyle\quad\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}(\mu_{x}^{\ast},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}^{\ast}))-\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})

\displaystyle=\mathscr{F}_{2}(\mu_{x}^{\ast},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}^{\ast}))-\mathscr{F}_{2}({\mu_{x}},{\mu_{z}})-\lambda\left(\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})-\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathscr{F}_{2}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))-\mathscr{F}_{2}({\mu_{x}},{\mu_{z}})-\lambda\left(\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})-\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{R^{2}(R+M)^{2}}{8\lambda\sigma_{1}}.

So we have proved the first claim.

Now we are going to prove the second claim.
By Eq. (
32
) and that
\frac{R^{2}(R+M)^{2}}{8\lambda_{0}\sigma_{1}}=\epsilon_{1}
, there is

\displaystyle\quad\mathscr{F}_{2}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})-\mathscr{F}_{2}(\mu_{x}^{\ast},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}^{\ast}))+\lambda_{0}\left(\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})-\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))\right)

\displaystyle\geq\mathscr{F}_{2}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})-\mathscr{F}_{2}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))+\lambda_{0}\left(\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})-\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))\right)\geq-\epsilon_{1}.

(33)

From the
\epsilon_{2}
-global-optimality of
({\mu_{x}}_{\lambda}^{(\epsilon_{2})},{\mu_{z}}_{\lambda}^{(\epsilon_{2})})
, there is

\displaystyle\quad\mathscr{F}_{2}({\mu_{x}}_{\lambda}^{(\epsilon_{2})},{\mu_{z}}_{\lambda}^{(\epsilon_{2})})+\lambda\left(\mathscr{F}_{1}({\mu_{x}}_{\lambda}^{(\epsilon_{2})},{\mu_{z}}_{\lambda}^{(\epsilon_{2})})-\mathscr{F}_{1}({\mu_{x}}_{\lambda}^{(\epsilon_{2})},\mu_{z}^{\ast}({\mu_{x}}_{\lambda}^{(\epsilon_{2})}))\right)-\epsilon_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\mathscr{F}_{2}(\mu_{x}^{\ast},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}^{\ast}))-\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{2}\quad\big(\text{ since }\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{x}^{\ast},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}^{\ast}))-\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{x}^{\ast},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}^{\ast}))=0\big)

\displaystyle\leq\mathscr{F}_{2}({\mu_{x}}_{\lambda}^{(\epsilon_{2})},{\mu_{z}}_{\lambda}^{(\epsilon_{2})})+\lambda_{0}\left(\mathscr{F}_{1}({\mu_{x}}_{\lambda}^{(\epsilon_{2})},{\mu_{z}}_{\lambda}^{(\epsilon_{2})})-\mathscr{F}_{1}({\mu_{x}}_{\lambda}^{(\epsilon_{2})},\mu_{z}^{\ast}({\mu_{x}}_{\lambda}^{(\epsilon_{2})}))\right)+\epsilon_{2}.

where the last inequality holds by Eq. (
33
).
Therefore, by rearranging the terms in the above inequality, we have

\displaystyle\quad(\lambda-\lambda_{0})\left(\mathscr{F}_{1}({\mu_{x}}_{\lambda}^{(\epsilon_{2})},{\mu_{z}}_{\lambda}^{(\epsilon_{2})})-\mathscr{F}_{1}({\mu_{x}}_{\lambda}^{(\epsilon_{2})},\mu_{z}^{\ast}({\mu_{x}}_{\lambda}^{(\epsilon_{2})}))\right)\leq\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{2}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\quad\varepsilon:=\mathscr{F}_{1}({\mu_{x}}_{\lambda}^{(\epsilon_{2})},{\mu_{z}}_{\lambda}^{(\epsilon_{2})})-\mathscr{F}_{1}({\mu_{x}}_{\lambda}^{(\epsilon_{2})},\mu_{z}^{\ast}({\mu_{x}}_{\lambda}^{(\epsilon_{2})}))\leq\frac{\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{2}}{\lambda-\lambda_{0}}.

Now we have proved that
({\mu_{x}}_{\lambda}^{(\epsilon_{2})},{\mu_{z}}_{\lambda}^{(\epsilon_{2})})
satisfy the constraint in (

ε

-constrained
).
In the meantime, consider
(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})
that are the global solution to the penalty formulation (

ε

-constrained
), we have

\displaystyle\quad\mathscr{F}_{2}({\mu_{x}}_{\lambda}^{(\epsilon_{2})},{\mu_{z}}_{\lambda}^{(\epsilon_{2})})-\mathscr{F}_{2}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})

\displaystyle\leq-\lambda\left(\mathscr{F}_{1}({\mu_{x}}_{\lambda}^{(\epsilon_{2})},{\mu_{z}}_{\lambda}^{(\epsilon_{2})})-\mathscr{F}_{1}({\mu_{x}}_{\lambda}^{(\epsilon_{2})},\mu_{z}^{\ast}({\mu_{x}}_{\lambda}^{(\epsilon_{2})})\right)+\lambda\left(\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})-\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})\right)+\epsilon_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\lambda(\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})-\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})-\varepsilon)+\epsilon_{2}\leq\epsilon_{2}.

The first inequality holds from the
\epsilon_{2}
-global-optimality of
({\mu_{x}}_{\lambda}^{(\epsilon_{2})},{\mu_{z}}_{\lambda}^{(\epsilon_{2})})
, and the last inequality holds from the fact that
(\mu_{x},\mu_{z})
are the global solution to the penalty formulation (

ε

-constrained
).
So we have proved that
({\mu_{x}}_{\lambda}^{(\epsilon_{2})},{\mu_{z}}_{\lambda}^{(\epsilon_{2})})
is the
\epsilon_{2}
-global optimum of (

ε

-constrained
), which concludes the proof of the second claim.

Now we are going to prove the third claim.
Let
(\mu_{x}^{(\epsilon_{3})},\mu_{z}^{(\epsilon_{3})})
be the
\epsilon_{3}
-global-solution to the constrained problem (

ε

-constrained
).
Then, we have

\displaystyle\left|\mathscr{F}_{2}(\mu_{x}^{(\epsilon_{3})},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}^{(\epsilon_{3})}))-\mathscr{F}_{2}(\mu_{x}^{(\epsilon_{3})},\mu_{z}^{(\epsilon_{3})})\right|

\displaystyle=\left|U_{2}(\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}^{(\epsilon_{3})}))-U_{2}(\mu_{z}^{(\epsilon_{3})})\right|

\displaystyle\leq R(R+M)\cdot\mathrm{TV}(\mu_{z}^{(\epsilon_{3})},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}^{(\epsilon_{3})})).

Next, since
\mu_{z}^{(\epsilon_{3})}
satisfies the constraint in (

ε

-constrained
), we have

\displaystyle\varepsilon\geq\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{x}^{(\epsilon_{3})},\mu_{z}^{(\epsilon_{3})})-\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{x}^{(\epsilon_{3})},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}^{(\epsilon_{3})}))\geq\sigma_{1}\mathrm{KL}(\mu_{z}^{(\epsilon_{3})},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}^{(\epsilon_{3})}))\geq 2\sigma_{1}\mathrm{TV}^{2}(\mu_{z}^{(\epsilon_{3})},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}^{(\epsilon_{3})})).

Combine the above two inequalities, we achieve

\displaystyle\left|\mathscr{F}_{2}(\mu_{x}^{(\epsilon_{3})},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}^{(\epsilon_{3})}))-\mathscr{F}_{2}(\mu_{x}^{(\epsilon_{3})},\mu_{z}^{(\epsilon_{3})})\right|\leq R(R+M)\sqrt{(2\sigma_{1})^{-1}\varepsilon}.

Note that
(\mu_{x}^{\ast},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}^{\ast}))
the global optimum of (
Bi-MFLD
) satisfies the constraint in (

ε

-constrained
). By definition of
\epsilon_{3}
-global-minimum, so we have

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}_{2}(\mu_{x}^{(\epsilon_{3})},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}^{(\epsilon_{3})}))-R(R+M)\sqrt{(2\sigma_{1})^{-1}\varepsilon}\leq\mathscr{F}_{2}(\mu_{x}^{(\epsilon_{3})},\mu_{z}^{(\epsilon_{3})})\leq\mathscr{F}_{2}(\mu_{x}^{\ast},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}^{\ast}))+\epsilon_{3}.

The proof is thus concluded.
∎

9.2
Proofs in
Section

5

9.2.1
Proof of
Theorem

5.12

For fixed
s\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
, the update scheme from time
s
to
s+1
of the outer loop in
Algorithm

2
is the following: let
\{\xi_{x,s}^{(i)}\}_{i=1}^{N_{x}}
be
N_{x}
i.i.d samples from
\mathcal{N}(0,\mathrm{Id}_{d_{x}})
,

\displaystyle x_{s+1}^{(i)}=x_{s}^{(i)}-\gamma\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{1}L_{\lambda}(\mu_{\mathscr{X},s},\tilde{\mu}_{\mathscr{Z},s},\mu_{\mathscr{Z},s})(\hat{x}_{s}^{(i)})+\sqrt{2\gamma\sigma_{2}}\xi_{x,s}^{(i)},\quad i=1,\ldots,N_{x},

(34)

Here,
\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{1}L_{\lambda}
denotes taking the Wasserstein gradient with respect to the first input of
L_{\lambda}
and its formula is given in Eq. (
4.2
).

Following the one-step interpolation technique from
77
;
73
, we define another system of particles with the initialization:
\hat{x}_{0}^{(i)}=x_{s}^{(i)}
(for all
i=1,\ldots,N_{x}
) and the update scheme: for
0\leq\tau\leq\gamma
and
i=1,\ldots,N_{x}
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\hat{x}_{\tau}^{(i)}

\displaystyle=-\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{1}L_{\lambda}(\hat{\mu}_{\mathscr{X},0},\tilde{\mu}_{\mathscr{Z},s},\mu_{\mathscr{Z},s})(\hat{x}_{0}^{(i)})\;\mathrm{d}\tau+\sqrt{2\sigma_{2}}\mathrm{d}W_{x,\tau}^{(i)},\quad\hat{\mu}_{\mathscr{X},0}=\frac{1}{N_{x}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{x}}\delta_{\hat{x}_{0}^{(i)}}.

(35)

Here,
W_{x,\tau}^{(i)}
is the
d_{x}
dimensional standard Brownian motion.
Then,
\{\hat{x}_{\gamma}^{(i)}\}_{i=1}^{N_{x}}
follow the same distribution as
\{x_{s+1}^{(i)}\}_{i=1}^{N_{x}}
.
Define
\hat{\mu}_{x,\tau}^{(N_{x})}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}((\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}})^{N_{x}})
as the joint distribution of
N_{x}
particles
\{\hat{x}_{\tau}^{(i)}\}_{i=1}^{N_{x}}
for the intermediate time
\tau\in[0,\gamma]
. From
66
, the corresponding Fokker plank equation of Eq. (
35
) is

\displaystyle\quad\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}\hat{\mu}_{x,\tau}^{(N_{x})}\left(\mathscr{X}\mid\hat{\mathscr{X}}_{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{x}}\nabla_{i}\cdot\left(\hat{\mu}_{x,\tau}^{(N_{x})}\left(\mathscr{X}\mid\hat{\mathscr{X}}_{0}\right)\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{1}L_{\lambda}(\hat{\mu}_{\mathscr{X},0},\tilde{\mu}_{\mathscr{Z},s},\mu_{\mathscr{Z},s})(x^{(i)})\right)+\sigma_{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{x}}\Delta_{i}\hat{\mu}_{x,\tau}^{(N_{x})}\left(\mathscr{X}\mid\hat{\mathscr{X}}_{0}\right).

(36)

Take the expectation over
\hat{\mathscr{X}}_{0}\sim\hat{\mu}_{x,0}^{(N_{x})}
and we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}\hat{\mu}_{x,\tau}^{(N_{x})}\left(\mathscr{X}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{x}}\nabla_{i}\cdot\left(\hat{\mu}_{x,\tau}^{(N_{x})}(\mathscr{X})\mathbb{E}_{\hat{\mathscr{X}}_{0}\mid\hat{\mathscr{X}}_{\tau}}[\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{1}L_{\lambda}(\hat{\mu}_{\mathscr{X},0},\tilde{\mu}_{\mathscr{Z},s},\mu_{\mathscr{Z},s})(\hat{x}_{0}^{(i)})\mid\hat{\mathscr{X}}_{\tau}=\mathscr{X}]\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\sigma_{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{x}}\Delta_{i}\hat{\mu}_{x,\tau}^{(N_{x})}\left(\mathscr{X}\right).

Recall that
\mu_{\ast}^{(N_{x})}=\arg\min_{\mu^{(N_{x})}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}((\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}})^{N_{x}})}\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}^{(N_{x})}(\mu^{(N_{x})})
for
\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}^{(N_{x})}
defined in Eq. (
5.2
). It satisfies

\displaystyle\mu_{\ast}^{(N_{x})}(\mathscr{X})

\displaystyle\propto\exp\left(-\frac{N_{x}}{\sigma_{2}}\Big(F_{2}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}}))+\lambda\cdot\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}}))-\lambda\cdot\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}}))\right).

(37)

For any
1\leq i\leq N_{x}
, denote
\nabla_{i}
as taking the derivative with respect to
x^{(i)}
for a mapping from
(\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}})^{N}
to
\mathbb{R}
. We have

\displaystyle\quad-\sigma_{2}\cdot\nabla_{i}\log(\mu_{\ast}^{(N_{x})}(\mathscr{X}))

\displaystyle=N_{x}\nabla_{i}F_{2}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}}))+\lambda\cdot N_{x}\nabla_{i}F_{1}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}}))+\lambda\sigma_{1}\cdot N_{x}\nabla_{i}\log(\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}}))

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\lambda\cdot N_{x}\nabla_{i}F_{1}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}}))-\lambda\sigma_{1}\cdot N_{x}\nabla_{i}\log(\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}}))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ast)}}{{=}}\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{1}F_{2}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}}))(x^{(i)})+\lambda\cdot\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{1}F_{1}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}}))(x^{(i)})-\lambda\cdot\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{1}F_{1}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}}))(x^{(i)})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{1}L_{\lambda}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}}))(x^{(i)}).

(38)

In
(\ast)
above, the gradient with respect to the nested mapping
\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}}),\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}})
vanish due to the optimality of
\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}})
and
\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}})
, which gives

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{2}F_{2}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}}))(\cdot)+\lambda\cdot\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{2}F_{1}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}}))(\cdot)+\lambda\sigma_{1}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nabla}\mathrm{Ent}(\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}}))(\cdot)

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{2}F_{1}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}}))(\cdot)+\sigma_{1}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nabla}\mathrm{Ent}(\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}}))(\cdot)

\displaystyle=0.

So we have, continuing from Eq. (
36
),

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}\hat{\mu}_{x,\tau}^{(N_{x})}\left(\mathscr{X}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{x}}\sigma_{2}\nabla_{i}\cdot\left(\hat{\mu}_{x,\tau}^{(N_{x})}(\mathscr{X})\left(\nabla_{i}\log\left(\frac{\hat{\mu}_{x,\tau}^{(N_{x})}}{\mu_{\ast}^{(N_{x})}}(\mathscr{X})\right)\right)\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=1}^{N_{x}}\nabla_{i}\cdot\left\{\hat{\mu}_{x,\tau}^{(N_{x})}(\mathscr{X})\left(\mathbb{E}_{\hat{\mathscr{X}}_{0}\mid\hat{\mathscr{X}}_{\tau}}[\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{1}L_{\lambda}(\hat{\mu}_{\mathscr{X},0},\tilde{\mu}_{\mathscr{Z},s},\mu_{\mathscr{Z},s})(\hat{x}_{0}^{(i)})\mid\hat{\mathscr{X}}_{\tau}=\mathscr{X}]\right.\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-\left.\left.\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{1}L_{\lambda}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}}))(x^{(i)})\right)\right\}.

(39)

Recall that
\mu_{z,s}^{(N_{z})}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}((\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}})^{N_{z}})
and
\tilde{\mu}_{z,s}^{(N_{z})}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}((\mathbb{R}^{d_{z}})^{N_{z}})
are the joint distributions of the
N_{z}
particles which are the output of the inner-loop algorithm
Algorithm

1
at time
s
. The corresponding empirical distributions are
\tilde{\mu}_{\mathscr{Z},s},\mu_{\mathscr{Z},s}
.
These distributions are fixed since
s
is fixed so they are independent of
\tau
.

Consider the time derivative with respect to
\tau
of
\mathbb{E}_{\mathscr{Z}\sim\mu_{z,s}^{(N_{z})},\;\tilde{\mathscr{Z}}\sim\tilde{\mu}_{z,s}^{(N_{z})}}[\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}^{(N_{x})}(\hat{\mu}_{x,\tau}^{(N_{x})})]
, where the expectation is taken with respect to the implicit dependence of
\hat{\mu}_{x,\tau}^{(N_{x})}
on the particles
\mathscr{Z}_{s}=\{z_{s}^{(i)}\}_{i=1}^{N_{z}}
(resp.
\tilde{\mathscr{Z}}_{s}=\{\tilde{z}_{s}^{(i)}\}_{i=1}^{N_{z}}
) of the empirical distributions
\tilde{\mu}_{\mathscr{Z},s}
(resp.
\mu_{\mathscr{Z},s}
) through the update scheme in Eq. (
35
).
We have

\displaystyle\quad\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}\left\{\mathbb{E}_{\mathscr{Z}\sim\mu_{z,s}^{(N_{z})},\;\tilde{\mathscr{Z}}\sim\tilde{\mu}_{z,s}^{(N_{z})}}\left[\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}^{(N_{x})}(\hat{\mu}_{x,\tau}^{(N_{x})})\right]\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathscr{Z},\tilde{\mathscr{Z}}}\left[\int\frac{\mathrm{d}\hat{\mu}_{x,\tau}^{(N_{x})}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}(\mathscr{X})\left(N_{x}F_{2}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}}))+\lambda\cdot N_{x}F_{1}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}}))+\lambda\sigma_{1}\cdot N_{x}\mathrm{Ent}(\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}}))\right.\right.

\displaystyle\qquad-\lambda\cdot N_{x}F_{1}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}}))-\lambda\sigma_{1}\cdot N_{x}\mathrm{Ent}(\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}}))+\left.\left.\sigma_{2}\log(\hat{\mu}_{x,\tau}^{(N_{x})})(\mathscr{X})\right)\;\mathrm{d}\mathscr{X}\right].

(40)

Next, we plug in the definition of
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}\hat{\mu}_{x,\tau}^{(N_{x})}
from Eq. (
39
) into the above equation. Also, we apply Eq. (
9.2.1
) and the integration by parts. Then, we obtain

\displaystyle\quad\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}\left\{\mathbb{E}_{\mathscr{Z}\sim\mu_{z,s}^{(N_{z})},\;\tilde{\mathscr{Z}}\sim\tilde{\mu}_{z,s}^{(N_{z})}}\left[\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}^{(N_{x})}(\hat{\mu}_{x,\tau}^{(N_{x})})\right]\right\}

\displaystyle=-\sigma_{2}^{2}\mathbb{E}_{\mathscr{Z},\tilde{\mathscr{Z}}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{N_{x}}\left\|\nabla_{i}\log\left(\frac{\hat{\mu}_{x,\tau}^{(N_{x})}}{\mu_{\ast}^{(N_{x})}}(\mathscr{X})\right)\right\|_{L^{2}\left(\hat{\mu}_{x,\tau}^{(N_{x})}\right)}^{2}\right]-\sigma_{2}\mathbb{E}_{\mathscr{Z},\tilde{\mathscr{Z}}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{N_{x}}\int\hat{\mu}_{x,\tau}^{(N_{x})}(\mathscr{X})\right.

\displaystyle\quad\left(\mathbb{E}_{\hat{\mathscr{X}}_{0}\mid\hat{\mathscr{X}}_{\tau}}[\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{1}L_{\lambda}(\hat{\mu}_{\mathscr{X},0},\tilde{\mu}_{\mathscr{Z},s},\mu_{\mathscr{Z},s})(\hat{x}_{0}^{(i)})\mid\hat{\mathscr{X}}_{\tau}=\mathscr{X}]-\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{1}L_{\lambda}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}}))(x^{(i)})\right)^{\top}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\nabla_{i}\log\left(\frac{\hat{\mu}_{x,\tau}^{(N_{x})}}{\mu_{\ast}^{(N_{x})}}(\mathscr{X})\right)\;\mathrm{d}\mathscr{X}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{\sigma_{2}^{2}}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\mathscr{Z},\tilde{\mathscr{Z}}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{N_{x}}\left\|\nabla_{i}\log\left(\frac{\hat{\mu}_{x,\tau}^{(N_{x})}}{\mu_{\ast}^{(N_{x})}}(\mathscr{X})\right)\right\|_{L^{2}\left(\hat{\mu}_{x,\tau}^{(N_{x})}\right)}^{2}\right]

(41)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{x}}\mathbb{E}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\hat{\mathscr{X}}_{0}\sim\hat{\mu}_{x,0}^{(N_{x})},\;\hat{\mathscr{X}}_{\tau}\sim\hat{\mu}_{x,\tau}^{(N_{x})}\\
\mathscr{Z}\sim\mu_{z,s}^{(N_{z})},\;\tilde{\mathscr{Z}}\sim\tilde{\mu}_{z,s}^{(N_{z})}\end{subarray}}\left[\left\|\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{1}L_{\lambda}(\hat{\mu}_{\mathscr{X},0},\tilde{\mu}_{\mathscr{Z},s},\mu_{\mathscr{Z},s})(\hat{x}_{0}^{(i)})\right.\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 150.00023pt\left.\left.-\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{1}L_{\lambda}\left(\hat{\mu}_{\mathscr{X},\tau},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X},\tau}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X},\tau})\right)(\hat{x}_{\tau}^{(i)})\right\|^{2}\right].

(42)

The last inequality holds by applying Cauchy-Schwartz inequality.
The first term in Eq. (
41
) can be upper bound by a defective uniform Log-Sobolev inequality proved in
5.10
:

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{\sigma_{2}^{2}}{2N_{x}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{x}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathscr{Z}\sim\mu_{z,s}^{(N_{z})},\;\tilde{\mathscr{Z}}\sim\tilde{\mu}_{z,s}^{(N_{z})}}\left[\left\|\nabla_{i}\log\left(\frac{\hat{\mu}_{x,\tau}^{(N_{x})}}{\mu_{\ast}^{(N_{x})}}(\mathscr{X})\right)\right\|_{L^{2}\left(\hat{\mu}_{x,\tau}^{(N_{x})}\right)}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{C_{\mathrm{LSI},x}\sigma_{2}}{4N_{x}}\left(\mathbb{E}_{\mathscr{Z}\sim\mu_{z,s}^{(N_{z})},\;\tilde{\mathscr{Z}}\sim\tilde{\mu}_{z,s}^{(N_{z})}}\left[\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}^{(N_{x})}\left(\hat{\mu}_{x,\tau}^{(N_{x})}\right)\right]-N_{x}\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}(\mu_{x,\lambda}^{\ast},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x,\lambda}^{\ast}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x,\lambda}^{\ast}))\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{\lambda C_{\mathrm{LSI},x}R^{2}\sigma_{2}\left(\frac{R}{2\sigma_{1}}+2\right)}{2N_{x}}+\frac{\mathfrak{c}^{2}C_{\mathrm{LSI},x}^{2}\sigma_{2}}{8}.

(43)

And the second term in Eq. (
41
) can be upper bounded by the following three upper bounds proved in
9.2
,
9.3
and
9.4
, respectively.
From
9.2
, we have, for
i=1,\ldots,N_{x}
,

\displaystyle\quad\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{1}L_{\lambda}(\mu_{\mathscr{X},\tau},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X},\tau}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X},\tau}))(\hat{x}_{\tau}^{(i)})-\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{1}L_{\lambda}(\mu_{\mathscr{X},\tau},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X},0}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X},0}))(\hat{x}_{\tau}^{(i)})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\lambda^{2}R^{6}}{\sigma_{1}}\left(\gamma^{2}\left(\zeta_{2}^{2}\frac{1}{N_{x}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{x}}\mathbb{E}[\|\hat{x}_{0}^{(i)}\|^{2}]+\lambda^{2}R^{2}\right)+\gamma\sigma_{2}d_{x}\right).

(44)

Here, the expectation on the left hand side is taken over the joint distribution of the particles
\{\hat{x}_{\tau}^{(i)}\}_{i=1}^{N_{x}}\sim\hat{\mu}_{x,\tau}^{(N_{x})}
and
\{\hat{x}_{0}^{(i)}\}_{i=1}^{N_{x}}\sim\hat{\mu}_{x,0}^{(N_{x})}
.
From
9.3
, we have, for
i=1,\ldots,N_{x}
,

\displaystyle\quad\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{1}L_{\lambda}(\mu_{\mathscr{X},0},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\hat{\mu}_{\mathscr{X},0}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\hat{\mu}_{\mathscr{X},0}))(\hat{x}_{0}^{(i)})-\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{1}L_{\lambda}(\mu_{\mathscr{X},\tau},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\hat{\mu}_{\mathscr{X},0}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\hat{\mu}_{\mathscr{X},0}))(\hat{x}_{\tau}^{(i)})\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq(2\lambda^{2}R^{4}+\zeta_{2}^{2})\left(\gamma^{2}\left(\zeta_{2}^{2}\frac{1}{N_{x}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{x}}\mathbb{E}[\|\hat{x}_{0}^{(i)}\|^{2}]+\lambda^{2}R^{2}\right)+\gamma\sigma_{2}d_{x}\right).

(45)

Here, the expectation is taken over the joint distribution of the particles
\{\hat{x}_{\tau}^{(i)}\}_{i=1}^{N_{x}}\sim\hat{\mu}_{x,\tau}^{(N_{x})}
and
\{\hat{x}_{0}^{(i)}\}_{i=1}^{N_{x}}\sim\hat{\mu}_{x,0}^{(N_{x})}
. From
9.4
, we have, for
i=1,\ldots,N_{x}
,

\displaystyle\quad\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{1}L_{\lambda}(\mu_{\mathscr{X},0},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\hat{\mu}_{\mathscr{X},0}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\hat{\mu}_{\mathscr{X},0}))(\hat{x}_{0}^{(i)})-\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{1}L_{\lambda}(\mu_{\mathscr{X},0},\tilde{\mu}_{\mathscr{Z},s},\mu_{\mathscr{Z},s})(\hat{x}_{0}^{(i)})\right\|^{2}\right]

(46)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\lambda^{2}R^{4}}{N_{z}}\left(\mathrm{KL}\left(\mu_{z,s}^{(N_{z})},(\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\hat{\mu}_{\mathscr{X},0}))^{\otimes N_{z}}\right)+\mathrm{KL}\left(\tilde{\mu}_{z,s}^{(N_{z})},(\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\hat{\mu}_{\mathscr{X},0}))^{\otimes N_{z}}\right)+1\right).

Here, the expectation is taken over the joint distribution of the particles
\{\tilde{z}_{s}^{(i)}\}_{i=1}^{N_{z}}\sim\tilde{\mu}_{z,s}^{(N_{z})}
and
\{z_{s}^{(i)}\}_{i=1}^{N_{z}}\sim\mu_{z,s}^{(N_{z})}
.

Now, we combine Eq. (
9.2.1
), Eq. (
9.2.1
), Eq. (
9.2.1
) and Eq. (
46
), and plug these bounds back to Eq. (
42
).
To simplify the notation, define

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\tau):=N_{x}^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{\mathscr{Z}\sim\mu_{z,s}^{(N_{z})},\;\tilde{\mathscr{Z}}\sim\tilde{\mu}_{z,s}^{(N_{z})}}\left[\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}^{(N_{x})}(\hat{\mu}_{x,\tau}^{(N_{x})}))\right]-\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}({\mu_{x}}_{\lambda}^{\ast},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}({\mu_{x}}_{\lambda}^{\ast}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}({\mu_{x}}_{\lambda}^{\ast})).

(47)

So we obtain,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}\mathcal{E}(\tau)

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{C_{\mathrm{LSI},x}\sigma_{2}}{4}\mathcal{E}(\tau)+\frac{\lambda C_{\mathrm{LSI},x}R^{2}\sigma_{2}(\frac{R}{\sigma_{1}}+2)}{N_{x}}+\frac{\mathfrak{c}^{2}C_{\mathrm{LSI},x}^{2}\sigma_{2}}{8}

\displaystyle\quad+(2\lambda^{2}R^{4}+\zeta_{2}^{2}+\frac{\lambda^{2}R^{6}}{\sigma_{1}})\left(\gamma^{2}\left(\zeta_{2}^{2}\frac{1}{N_{x}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{x}}\mathbb{E}[\|\hat{x}_{0}^{(i)}\|^{2}]+\lambda^{2}R^{2}\right)+2\gamma\sigma_{2}d_{x}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\lambda^{2}R^{4}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\mathrm{KL}\left(\mu_{z}^{(N_{z})},{(\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\hat{\mu}_{\mathscr{X},0}))}^{\otimes N_{z}}\right)}{N_{z}}}+\sqrt{\frac{\mathrm{KL}\left(\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{(N_{z})},{(\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\hat{\mu}_{\mathscr{X},0}))}^{\otimes N_{z}}\right)}{N_{z}}}+1\right).

Since
\{\hat{x}_{0}^{(i)}\}_{i=1}^{N_{x}}=\{x_{s}^{(i)}\}_{i=1}^{N_{x}}
, we have
\mathbb{E}[\|\hat{x}_{0}^{(i)}\|^{2}]=\mathbb{E}[\|x_{s}^{(i)}\|^{2}]
. Also from the uniform second moment bound proved in
10.1
provided that
\gamma\leq\frac{1}{\zeta_{2}}
, we have
\zeta_{2}^{2}\mathbb{E}[\|x_{s}^{(i)}\|^{2}]\leq\zeta_{2}^{2}\mathbb{E}[\|x_{0}^{(i)}\|^{2}]+\lambda^{2}R^{2}+2\zeta_{2}\sigma_{2}d_{x}
. Notice that
\frac{1}{N_{x}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{x}}\mathbb{E}[\|x_{0}^{(i)}\|^{2}]=\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{x,0}}[\|x\|^{2}]
which is the average second moment of all particles at initialization. So we have

\displaystyle\gamma^{2}\left(\zeta_{2}^{2}\frac{1}{N_{x}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{x}}\mathbb{E}[\|\hat{x}_{0}^{(i)}\|^{2}]+\lambda^{2}R^{2}\right)+2\gamma\sigma_{2}d_{x}

\displaystyle\leq\gamma^{2}(\zeta_{2}^{2}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{x,0}}[\|x\|^{2}]+2\lambda^{2}R^{2}+\zeta_{2}\sigma_{2}d_{x})+2\gamma\sigma_{2}d_{x}

\displaystyle\leq\gamma^{2}(\zeta_{2}^{2}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{x,0}}[\|x\|^{2}]+2\lambda^{2}R^{2})+3\gamma\sigma_{2}d_{x}.

Recall that for any
s\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
,
\mu_{z,s}^{(N_{z})}
and
\tilde{\mu}_{z,s}^{(N_{z})}
denote the joint
distribution of the
N_{z}
particles which are the output of the inner-loop algorithm
InnerLoop
(
\mu_{\mathscr{X},s}
,
T
,
\alpha
,
\beta
,
\lambda
,
\sigma_{1}
), detailed in
Algorithm

1
and
\mu_{\mathscr{X},s}=\hat{\mu}_{\mathscr{X},0}
.
As a result, let

\mathfrak{KL}=\mathrm{KL}(\mu_{z,s}^{(N_{z})},(\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X},s})^{\otimes N_{z}})
and
\tilde{\mathfrak{KL}}=\mathrm{KL}(\tilde{\mu}_{z,s}^{(N_{z})},(\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X},s})^{\otimes N_{z}})
as defined in the statement of
Theorem

5.12
that represent the error from the inner-loop algorithm. Both terms are upper bounded in
5.3
and
5.4
respectively.
Next, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}(\mathcal{E}(\tau)-\ldots)

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{C_{\mathrm{LSI},x}\sigma_{2}}{4}\Bigg(\mathcal{E}(\tau)-\frac{\lambda R^{2}(\frac{R}{\sigma_{1}}+2)}{N_{x}}-\mathfrak{c}^{2}C_{\mathrm{LSI},x}-\left(\frac{\sigma_{2}C_{\mathrm{LSI},x}}{4}\right)^{-1}\lambda^{2}R^{4}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\mathfrak{KL}}{N_{z}}}+\sqrt{\frac{\tilde{\mathfrak{KL}}}{N_{z}}}+\frac{1}{N_{z}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt-\left(\frac{\sigma_{2}C_{\mathrm{LSI},x}}{4}\right)^{-1}(2\lambda^{2}R^{4}+\zeta_{2}^{2}+\frac{\lambda^{2}R^{6}}{\sigma_{1}})\left(\gamma^{2}(\zeta_{2}^{2}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{x,0}}[\|x\|^{2}]+2\lambda^{2}R^{2})+3\gamma\sigma_{2}d_{x}\right)\Bigg).

Here,
\ldots
represents the same quantity in the large bracket of the right hand side.
Since
\{\hat{x}_{0}^{(i)}\}_{i=1}^{N_{x}}=\{x_{s}^{(i)}\}_{i=1}^{N_{x}}
and
\{\hat{x}_{\gamma}^{(i)}\}_{i=1}^{N_{x}}=\{x_{s+1}^{(i)}\}_{i=1}^{N_{x}}
by construction of the one-step interpolation particle system defined in Eq. (
35
).
So we have
\hat{\mu}_{x,0}^{(N_{x})}=\mu_{x,s}^{(N_{x})}
and
\hat{\mu}_{x,\gamma}^{(N_{x})}=\mu_{x,s+1}^{(N_{x})}
and consequently
\mathcal{E}(0)=\mathcal{H}(s)
and
\mathcal{E}(\gamma)=\mathcal{H}(s+1)
for
\mathcal{H}
defined in the statement of the proposition.
Hence, by integrating the above equation from
0
to
\gamma
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(s+1)-\ldots

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\frac{\sigma_{2}C_{\mathrm{LSI},x}\gamma}{4}\right)\Bigg(\mathcal{H}(s)-\frac{\lambda R^{2}(\frac{R}{\sigma_{1}}+1)}{N_{x}}-\mathfrak{c}^{2}C_{\mathrm{LSI},x}

\displaystyle\hskip-56.9055pt-\left(\frac{\sigma_{2}C_{\mathrm{LSI},x}}{4}\right)^{-1}\lambda^{2}R^{4}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\mathfrak{KL}}{N_{z}}}+\sqrt{\frac{\tilde{\mathfrak{KL}}}{N_{z}}}+\frac{1}{N_{z}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt-\left(\frac{\sigma_{2}C_{\mathrm{LSI},x}}{4}\right)^{-1}(2\lambda^{2}R^{4}+\zeta_{2}^{2}+\frac{\lambda^{2}R^{6}}{\sigma_{1}})\left(\gamma^{2}(\zeta_{2}^{2}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{x,0}}[\|x\|^{2}]+2\lambda^{2}R^{2})+3\gamma\sigma_{2}d_{x}\right)\Bigg).

Since the above equation holds for any
s\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(S)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\frac{\sigma_{2}C_{\mathrm{LSI},x}S\gamma}{4}\right)\mathcal{H}(0)+\frac{\lambda R^{2}(\frac{R}{\sigma_{1}}+1)}{N_{x}}+\mathfrak{c}^{2}C_{\mathrm{LSI},x}+\frac{\lambda^{2}R^{4}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\mathfrak{KL}}{N_{z}}}+\sqrt{\frac{\tilde{\mathfrak{KL}}}{N_{z}}}+\frac{1}{N_{z}}\right)}{\sigma_{2}C_{\mathrm{LSI},x}}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\lambda^{2}R^{4}+\zeta_{2}^{2}+\frac{\lambda^{2}R^{6}}{\sigma_{1}}}{\sigma_{2}C_{\mathrm{LSI},x}}\left(\gamma^{2}(\zeta_{2}^{2}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{x,0}}[\|x\|^{2}]+\lambda^{2}R^{2})+\gamma\sigma_{2}d_{x}\right).

In the last step, we omit all the positive scalar coefficients like
2
,
3
to simplify the presentation.

9.2.2
Proof of
5.8

Proof of
5.8
.

From the partial linear convexity of
\mu_{z}\mapsto L_{\lambda}(\mu_{x},\tilde{\mu}_{z},\mu_{z})
for fixed
\tilde{\mu}_{z},\mu_{z}
proved in
4.8
, we have

\displaystyle\hskip-10.0pt\int\delta_{\mu_{x}}L_{\lambda}(\mu_{x}^{\prime},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}^{\prime}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}^{\prime}))(x)\;\mathrm{d}(\mu_{x}^{\prime}-\mu_{x})\geq L_{\lambda}(\mu_{x}^{\prime},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}^{\prime}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}^{\prime}))-L_{\lambda}(\mu_{x},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}^{\prime}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}^{\prime})).

(48)

The optimality of
\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}^{\prime})
, implies that
L_{\lambda}(\mu_{x},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}^{\prime}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}^{\prime}))\leq L_{\lambda}(\mu_{x},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}^{\prime}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))
and hence,

\displaystyle\eqref{eq:delta_mu_x}\geq L_{\lambda}(\mu_{x}^{\prime},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}^{\prime}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}^{\prime}))-L_{\lambda}(\mu_{x},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}^{\prime}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})).

Finally, by
10.2
where
U_{\lambda}
defined in Eq. (
52
) is
L_{\lambda}
excluding the
\ell_{2}
regularization term
\frac{\zeta_{2}}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{x}}[\|x\|^{2}]
, we have

(
48
)

\displaystyle\geq L_{\lambda}(\mu_{x}^{\prime},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}^{\prime}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}^{\prime}))-L_{\lambda}(\mu_{x},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}^{\prime}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))

\displaystyle=U_{\lambda}(\mu_{x}^{\prime},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}^{\prime}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}^{\prime}))-U_{\lambda}(\mu_{x},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}^{\prime}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))+\frac{\zeta_{2}}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{x}}[\|x\|^{2}]-\frac{\zeta_{2}}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{x}^{\prime}}[\|x\|^{2}]

\displaystyle\geq U_{\lambda}(\mu_{x}^{\prime},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}^{\prime}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}^{\prime}))-U_{\lambda}(\mu_{x},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))-\frac{R\lambda}{4\sigma_{1}}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\left(\smallint\Psi_{\mathbf{a}}\;\mathrm{d}(\mu_{x}-\mu_{x}^{\prime})\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{\zeta_{2}}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{x}}[\|x\|^{2}]-\frac{\zeta_{2}}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{x}^{\prime}}[\|x\|^{2}]

\displaystyle=L_{\lambda}(\mu_{x}^{\prime},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}^{\prime}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}^{\prime}))-L_{\lambda}(\mu_{x},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))-\frac{R\lambda}{4\sigma_{1}}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\left(\smallint\Psi_{\mathbf{a}}\;\mathrm{d}(\mu_{x}-\mu_{x}^{\prime})\right)^{2}\right].

The proof is thus concluded.
∎

9.2.3
Proof of
5.9

Proof of
5.9
.

From Eq. (
10
), for any
\mu_{x}^{(N_{x})}
, we have

\displaystyle\quad N_{x}^{-1}\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}^{(N_{x})}(\mu_{x}^{(N_{x})})-\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}(\mu_{x,\lambda}^{\ast},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{*}(\mu_{x,\lambda}^{\ast}),\mu_{z}^{*}(\mu_{x,\lambda}^{\ast}))

\displaystyle=N_{x}^{-1}\sigma_{2}\mathrm{KL}(\mu_{x}^{(N_{x})},(\mu_{x,\lambda}^{\ast})^{\otimes N_{x}})+\mathbb{E}_{\mathscr{X}\sim\mu_{x}^{(N_{x})}}\left[B_{L_{\lambda}}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}},\mu_{x,\lambda}^{\ast})\right].

From
5.8
and Proposition 1 of
57
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathscr{X}\sim\mu_{x}^{(N_{x})}}\left[B_{L_{\lambda}}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}},\mu_{x,\lambda}^{\ast})\right]

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{R\lambda}{4\sigma_{1}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathscr{X}\sim\mu_{x}^{(N_{x})}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\left(\smallint\Psi_{\mathbf{a}}\;\mathrm{d}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}}-\mu_{x,\lambda}^{\ast})\right)^{2}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{R\lambda}{4\sigma_{1}}\left(8R^{2}\sqrt{N_{x}^{-1}\mathrm{KL}(\mu_{x}^{(N_{x})},(\mu_{x,\lambda}^{\ast})^{\otimes N_{x}})}+\frac{4R^{2}}{N_{x}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{2R^{3}\lambda}{\sigma_{1}}\left(\mathfrak{c}^{-1}N_{x}^{-1}\mathrm{KL}(\mu_{x}^{(N_{x})},(\mu_{x,\lambda}^{\ast})^{\otimes N_{x}})+\mathfrak{c}\right)-\frac{R^{3}\lambda}{\sigma_{1}N_{x}}.

The last line holds by the fact that

\displaystyle\sqrt{N_{x}^{-1}\mathrm{KL}(\mu_{x}^{(N_{x})},(\mu_{x,\lambda}^{\ast})^{\otimes N_{x}})}\leq\mathfrak{c}^{-1}N_{x}^{-1}\mathrm{KL}(\mu_{x}^{(N_{x})},(\mu_{x,\lambda}^{\ast})^{\otimes N_{x}})+\mathfrak{c}.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\quad N_{x}^{-1}\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}^{(N_{x})}(\mu_{x}^{(N_{x})})-\mathscr{L}_{\lambda}(\mu_{x,\lambda}^{\ast},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{*}(\mu_{x,\lambda}^{\ast}),\mu_{z}^{*}(\mu_{x,\lambda}^{\ast}))

\displaystyle\geq N_{x}^{-1}\sigma_{2}\mathrm{KL}(\mu_{x}^{(N_{x})},(\mu_{x,\lambda}^{\ast})^{\otimes N_{x}})-N_{x}^{-1}\frac{2R^{3}\lambda}{\sigma_{1}\mathfrak{c}}\mathrm{KL}(\mu_{x}^{(N_{x})},(\mu_{x,\lambda}^{\ast})^{\otimes N_{x}})-\frac{2R^{3}\lambda}{\sigma_{1}}\mathfrak{c}-\frac{R^{3}\lambda}{N_{x}\sigma_{1}}

\displaystyle\geq N_{x}^{-1}\left(\sigma_{2}-\frac{2R^{3}\lambda}{\sigma_{1}\mathfrak{c}}\right)\mathrm{KL}(\mu_{x}^{(N_{x})},(\mu_{x,\lambda}^{\ast})^{\otimes N_{x}})-\frac{2R^{3}\lambda}{\sigma_{1}}\mathfrak{c}-\frac{R^{3}\lambda}{N_{x}\sigma_{1}}

\displaystyle\geq N_{x}^{-1}\frac{\sigma_{2}}{2}\mathrm{KL}(\mu_{x}^{(N_{x})},(\mu_{x,\lambda}^{\ast})^{\otimes N_{x}})-\frac{2R^{3}\lambda}{\sigma_{1}}\mathfrak{c}-\frac{R^{3}\lambda}{N_{x}\sigma_{1}}.

The last inequality holds by the condition that
\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\mathfrak{c}>4R^{3}\lambda
.
∎

9.2.4
Proof of
5.10

Proof of
5.10
.

We follow the leave-one-out argument from
57
which is a refinement over previous leave-one-out arguments in
13
and
73
.
Recall from Eq. (
9.2.1
) that
-\sigma_{2}\cdot\nabla_{i}\log(\mu_{\ast}^{(N_{x})}(\mathscr{X}))=\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{1}L_{\lambda}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}}))(x^{(i)})
due to the optimality of
\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}})
and
\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}})
. We start with the fisher information,

\displaystyle\quad-\mathbb{E}_{\mathscr{X}\sim\mu_{x}^{(N_{x})}}\left[\left\|\nabla\log\left(\frac{\mu_{x}^{(N_{x})}}{\mu_{\ast}^{(N_{x})}}(\mathscr{X})\right)\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i=1}^{N_{x}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathscr{X}\sim\mu_{x}^{(N_{x})}}\left[\left\|\nabla_{i}\log\left(\frac{\mu_{x}^{(N_{x})}}{\mu_{\ast}^{(N_{x})}}(\mathscr{X})\right)\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i=1}^{N_{x}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathscr{X}\sim\mu_{x}^{(N_{x})}}\left[\left\|\nabla_{i}\log\left(\mu_{x}^{(N_{x})}(\mathscr{X})\right)+\frac{1}{\sigma_{2}}\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{1}L_{\lambda}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}}))(x^{(i)})\right\|^{2}\right]

(49)

Given
N_{x}
particles
\mathscr{X}
with a joint distribution
\mu_{x}^{(N_{x})}
, denote by
P_{x^{(i)}\mid\mathscr{X}_{-i}}
the conditional law of
x^{(i)}
conditioned by
\mathscr{X}_{-i}
and denote by
P_{\mathscr{X}_{-i}}
the marginal law of
\mathscr{X}_{-i}
.
Then, it holds that

\displaystyle\quad\nabla_{i}\log\left(\mu_{x}^{(N_{x})}(\mathscr{X})\right)=\frac{\nabla_{i}\left(P_{x^{(i)}\mid\mathscr{X}_{-i}}(x^{(i)})P_{\mathscr{X}_{-i}}\left(\mathscr{X}_{-i}\right)\right)}{P_{x^{(i)}\mid\mathscr{X}_{-i}}(x^{(i)})P_{\mathscr{X}_{-i}}\left(\mathscr{X}_{-i}\right)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\nabla_{i}P_{x^{(i)}\mid\mathscr{X}_{-i}}\left(x^{(i)}\right)}{P_{x^{(i)}\mid\mathscr{X}_{-i}}\left(x^{(i)}\right)}=\nabla_{i}\log\left(P_{x^{(i)}\mid\mathscr{X}_{-i}}\left(x^{(i)}\right)\right).

Denote by
\mu_{x\cup\mathscr{X}_{-i}}
as the empirical distribution
\mu_{x\cup\mathscr{X}_{-i}}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j\neq i}\delta_{x^{(j)}}+\frac{1}{N}\delta_{x}
which augments the leave-one-out distribution with a new different sample
x
.
Define another proximal Gibbs distribution

\displaystyle\underline{\mu}_{\mathscr{X},i\mid-i}^{(N_{x})}(x\mid\mathscr{X}_{-i})

\displaystyle\propto\exp\left(-\frac{N_{x}}{\sigma_{2}}\Big(F_{2}(\mu_{x\cup\mathscr{X}_{-i}},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x\cup\mathscr{X}_{-i}})+\lambda\cdot F_{1}(\mu_{x\cup\mathscr{X}_{-i}},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x\cup\mathscr{X}_{-i}}))\right.

(50)

\displaystyle+\lambda\sigma_{1}\cdot\mathrm{Ent}(\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x\cup\mathscr{X}_{-i}}))-\lambda\cdot F_{1}(\mu_{x\cup\mathscr{X}_{-i}},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x\cup\mathscr{X}_{-i}}))-\lambda\sigma_{1}\cdot\mathrm{Ent}(\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x\cup\mathscr{X}_{-i}}))\Big)\Bigg).

Let
\nabla_{x,1}F_{2}(\mu_{x\cup\mathscr{X}_{-i}},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x\cup\mathscr{X}_{-i}})
mean taking the derivative of
F_{2}(\mu_{x\cup\mathscr{X}_{-i}},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x\cup\mathscr{X}_{-i}}))
with respect to the augmented new sample
x
in the first argument. From the optimality of
\mu_{z}^{\ast},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}
and the same derivations as in Eq. (
9.2.1
), we have

\displaystyle\quad\sigma_{2}\cdot\nabla\log\underline{\mu}_{\mathscr{X},i\mid-i}^{(N_{x})}(x\mid\mathscr{X}_{-i})=-N_{x}\nabla_{x,1}F_{2}(\mu_{x\cup\mathscr{X}_{-i}},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x\cup\mathscr{X}_{-i}})-\lambda\cdot N_{x}\nabla_{x,1}F_{1}(\mu_{x\cup\mathscr{X}_{-i}},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x\cup\mathscr{X}_{-i}})

\displaystyle\qquad-\lambda\cdot N_{x}\nabla_{x,1}F_{1}(\mu_{x\cup\mathscr{X}_{-i}},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x\cup\mathscr{X}_{-i}})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{1}L_{\lambda}(\mu_{x\cup\mathscr{X}_{-i}},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x\cup\mathscr{X}_{-i}}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x\cup\mathscr{X}_{-i}}))(x).

Here,
L_{\lambda}
is defined in Eq. (
20
).
An immediate consequence of the above derivations is that, taking
x=x^{(i)}
, then
\sigma_{2}\nabla\log\underline{\mu}_{\mathscr{X},i\mid-i}^{(N_{x})}(x^{(i)}\mid\mathscr{X}_{-i})=\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{1}L_{\lambda}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}}))(x^{(i)})
.
Also, notice that
\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{1}F_{2}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}}))(x)=\zeta_{2}x
, so from the same argument as in
5.1
, the proximal Gibbs distribution
\underline{\mu}_{\mathscr{X},i\mid-i}^{(N_{x})}
satisfies a Log-Sobolev inequality with constant
C_{\mathrm{LSI},x}
.
So we can proceed from Eq. (
49
) above to obtain

(
49
)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i=1}^{N_{x}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathscr{X}_{-i}\sim P_{\mathscr{X}_{-i}}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{x^{(i)}\sim P_{x^{(i)}\mid\mathscr{X}_{-i}}}\left[\left\|\nabla_{i}\log\left(P_{x^{(i)}\mid\mathscr{X}_{-i}}(x^{(i)})\right)+\nabla\log\underline{\mu}_{\mathscr{X},i\mid-i}^{(N_{x})}(x^{(i)}\mid\mathscr{X}_{-i})\right\|^{2}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\leq-2C_{\mathrm{LSI},x}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{x}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathscr{X}_{-i}\sim P_{\mathscr{X}_{-i}}}\left[\mathrm{KL}\left(P_{x^{(i)}\mid\mathscr{X}_{-i}},\;\underline{\mu}_{\mathscr{X},i\mid-i}^{(N_{x})}\right)\right].

(51)

Recall the definition of
L_{\lambda}
in Eq. (
20
),

\displaystyle\quad L_{\lambda}(\mu_{x},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))

\displaystyle=F_{2}(\mu_{x},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))+\lambda\cdot\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{x},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))-\lambda\cdot\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))

\displaystyle\hskip-20.0pt=U_{2}(\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))+\lambda\cdot\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{x},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))-\lambda\cdot\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))+\frac{\zeta_{2}}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{x}}[\|x\|^{2}]

\displaystyle\hskip-20.0pt=:U_{\lambda}(\mu_{x},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))+\frac{\zeta_{2}}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{x}}[\|x\|^{2}].

(52)

Consider the first variation of
U_{\lambda}
with respect to
\mu_{x}
, denoted as
\delta_{\mu_{x}}U_{\lambda}
, which is a mapping from
\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}}\to\mathbb{R}
.
From the optimality of
\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})
and
\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})
, we have

\displaystyle\delta_{\mu_{x}}\Big(U_{2}(\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))+\lambda\cdot F_{1}(\mu_{x},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))+\lambda\sigma_{1}\cdot\mathrm{Ent}\left(\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})\right)\Big)(x)

\displaystyle=\lambda\cdot\delta_{1}F_{1}(\mu_{x},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))(x)+\text{const}

\displaystyle=\lambda\cdot\delta_{1}U_{1}(\mu_{x},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))(x)+\text{const},

\displaystyle\delta_{\mu_{x}}\Big(F_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))+\sigma_{1}\mathrm{Ent}\left(\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})\right)\Big)(x)

\displaystyle=\delta_{1}F_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))(x)+\text{const}

\displaystyle=\delta_{1}U_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))(x)+\text{const}.

Here, the constants are independent of
\mu_{x},x
. Hence, we can conclude that the first variation of
U_{\lambda}
with respect to
\mu_{x}
is:

\displaystyle\delta_{\mu_{x}}U_{\lambda}(\mu_{x},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))(\cdot)=\lambda\cdot\delta_{1}U_{1}(\mu_{x},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))(\cdot)-\lambda\cdot\delta_{1}U_{1}(\mu_{x},\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))(\cdot)+\text{const}.

(53)

Based on the partial convexity of
U_{1}
and
U_{2}
proved in
9.5
, we can see that the mapping from
\mu_{x}\mapsto U_{\lambda}(\mu_{x},\tilde{\mu}_{z},\mu_{z})
is convex if the latter
\tilde{\mu}_{z},\mu_{z}
are independent of
\mu_{x}
. Unfortunately however, this is not the case here as both
\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x})
explicitly depends on
\mu_{x}
. As a result, the mapping
\mu_{x}\mapsto U_{\lambda}(\mu_{x},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x}))
is
not convex
, and we must account carefully for this lack of convexity in our analysis.

By the optimality of the proximal Gibbs distribution
\underline{\mu}_{\mathscr{X},i\mid-i}^{(N_{x})}
defined in Eq. (
50
), we have

\displaystyle\underline{\mu}_{\mathscr{X},i\mid-i}^{(N_{x})}(x\mid\mathscr{X}_{-i})\propto\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\sigma_{2}}\delta_{\mu_{x}}L_{\lambda}\Big(\mu_{x\cup\mathscr{X}_{-i}},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x\cup\mathscr{X}_{-i}}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x\cup\mathscr{X}_{-i}})\Big)(x)\right)

\displaystyle=\underset{\mu_{x}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}})}{\operatorname{argmin}}\left\{\int\delta_{\mu_{x}}U_{\lambda}\left(\mu_{x\cup\mathscr{X}_{-i}},\;\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x\cup\mathscr{X}_{-i}}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x\cup\mathscr{X}_{-i}}))\right)(x)\;\mathrm{d}\mu_{x}(x)+\frac{\zeta_{2}}{2}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{x}}\left[\|x\|^{2}\right]+\sigma\operatorname{Ent}(\mu_{x})\right\}

\displaystyle=\underset{\mu_{x}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}})}{\operatorname{argmin}}\left\{\int\delta_{\mu_{x}}U_{\lambda}\left(\mu_{x\cup\mathscr{X}_{-i}},\;\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x\cup\mathscr{X}_{-i}}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x\cup\mathscr{X}_{-i}}))\right)(x)\;\mathrm{d}\mu_{x}(x)+\sigma_{2}\mathrm{KL}(\mu_{x},\nu)\right\},

(54)

Here,
\nu(x)\propto\exp(-\frac{\zeta_{2}}{2\sigma_{2}}\|x\|^{2})
is a Gaussian distribution.
To proceed, we obtain the following lower bound of the KL divergence in Eq. (
51
),

\displaystyle\sigma_{2}\mathrm{KL}\left(P_{x^{(i)}\mid\mathscr{X}_{-i}},\underline{\mu}_{\mathscr{X},i\mid-i}^{(N_{x})}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int\delta U_{\lambda}\left(\mu_{x\cup\mathscr{X}_{-i}},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x\cup\mathscr{X}_{-i}}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x\cup\mathscr{X}_{-i}}))\right)(x)\left(P_{x^{(i)}\mid\mathscr{X}_{-i}}-\underline{\mu}_{\mathscr{X},i\mid-i}^{(N_{x})}\right)(x)\;\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\sigma_{2}\mathrm{KL}\left(P_{x^{(i)}\mid\mathscr{X}_{-i}},\nu\right)-\sigma_{2}\mathrm{KL}\left(\underline{\mu}_{\mathscr{X},i\mid-i}^{(N_{x})},\nu\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\int\delta U_{\lambda}\left(\mu_{x\cup\mathscr{X}_{-i}},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x\cup\mathscr{X}_{-i}}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x\cup\mathscr{X}_{-i}})\right)(x)\left(P_{x^{(i)}\mid\mathscr{X}_{-i}}-\mu_{x,\lambda}^{\ast}\right)(x)\;\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\sigma_{2}\mathrm{KL}\left(P_{x^{(i)}\mid\mathscr{X}_{-i}},\nu\right)-\sigma_{2}\mathrm{KL}\left(\mu_{x,\lambda}^{\ast},\nu\right).

(55)

The last inequality holds by the optimality of the proximal Gibbs distribution
\underline{\mu}_{\mathscr{X},i\mid-i}^{(N_{x})}
.
Take the expectation
\mathbb{E}_{\mathscr{X}_{-i}\sim P_{\mathscr{X}_{-i}}}
with respect to the first term of the right hand side in Eq. (
55
) above,
we then proceed to obtain a further lower bound by the following:

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N_{x}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathscr{X}_{-i}\sim P_{\mathscr{X}_{-i}}}\left[\int\delta_{\mu_{x}}U_{\lambda}\left(\mu_{x\cup\mathscr{X}_{-i}},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x\cup\mathscr{X}_{-i}}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x\cup\mathscr{X}_{-i}})\right)(x)\left(P_{x^{(i)}\mid\mathscr{X}_{-i}}-\mu_{x,\lambda}^{\ast}\right)(x)\;\mathrm{d}x\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N_{x}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathscr{X}\sim\mu_{x}^{(N_{x})}}\left[\delta_{\mu_{x}}U_{\lambda}\left(\mu_{\mathscr{X}},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}})\right)(x^{(i)})-\int\delta_{\mu_{x}}U_{\lambda}\left(\mu_{x\cup\mathscr{X}_{-i}},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x\cup\mathscr{X}_{-i}}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{x\cup\mathscr{X}_{-i}})\right)(x)\;\mathrm{d}\mu_{x,\lambda}^{\ast}(x)\right]

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N_{x}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathscr{X}\sim\mu_{x}^{(N_{x})}}\left[\int\delta_{\mu_{x}}U_{\lambda}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}}))(x)\;\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathscr{X}}(x)-\int\delta_{\mu_{x}}U_{\lambda}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}},\tilde{\mu}_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}}),\mu_{z}^{\ast}(\mu_{\mathscr{X}}))(x)\;\mathrm{d}\mu_{x,\lambda}^{\ast}(x)\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt+2\lambda\left(R+\sqrt{\frac{2}{\sigma_{1}}}R^{2}\right)

(56)

\displaystyle=
